#5084. Crypto PACs eye 2028 ‘new map’ as Democrats take Congress

Crypto super PACs spent more than $176 million on the midterms, but the Democratic sweep raises the prospect of tougher industry scrutiny.

Two years after crypto money helped drive Sherrod Brown out of the Senate, roughly $64 million couldn’t keep him from returning.

Brown’s victory over Republican Jon Husted in Ohio delivered the industry its biggest electoral loss of the 2026 midterms as Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress. The sweep also puts Sen. Elizabeth Warren, one of crypto’s fiercest critics, in line to chair the Senate Banking Committee.

Crypto super PACs spent about $176 million across 100 seats during the election cycle, including primaries, according to Reuters, up from more than $130 million in 2024. Fairshake and its affiliated PACs accounted for $154.5 million across 89 races.

“The next Congress will bring a fundamentally different environment for crypto in Washington, and the industry should expect significantly more scrutiny,” Kyle Bligen, executive director at the Decentralization Research Center, told Cointelegraph.

Fairshake, meanwhile, is left with more than $67 million heading to the 2028 elections. “Newly elected and current members will have a choice: protect consumers and American jobs or fight progress,” spokesperson Geoff Vetter told Cointelegraph in an email. “We will be watching and ‘28 is a new map.”

Ohio was the industry’s biggest bet as crypto PACs put more than $64 million into keeping Senator Jon Husted in office. His opponent, former Sen. Sherrod Brown, won the special election to complete former Sen. JD Vance’s term, which ends in January 2029. He could seek a senior role on the Senate Agriculture Committee, which oversees the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC).

Brown’s comeback came two years after crypto’s biggest win in 2024, when Fairshake spent $40 million to help Bernie Moreno unseat him in Ohio’s other Senate seat. Some industry leaders treated that result as a warning to Congress. Gemini exchange co-founder Cameron Winklevoss declared on X: “This is what happens when you mess with the crypto army”.

This year, crypto spending was part of a much larger flood of outside money. Independent expenditures in the race topped $350 million, while Brown turned the crypto spending into a campaign theme, writing “I’ll be damned if crypto billionaires decide this race,” in a campaign message.

The loss in Ohio may weaken the perception that defying crypto PACs carries an electoral cost in Washington. Before the vote, a Republican crypto lobbyist told Politico that a Brown win would “neuter Fairshake’s perceived Sword of Damocles.”

Nebraska, meanwhile, offered crypto PACs a significant win, with Sen. Pete Ricketts retaining his seat after Fairshake affiliate Defend American Jobs spent $13.5 million opposing independent challenger Dan Osborn.

“As the biggest spender in the Nebraska Senate race, we’re proud to have helped Sen. Ricketts weather the waves of a hard-fought cycle,” wrote Vetter. “Fairshake played a meaningful role in winning competitive races across the country in 2026 and we will do it again in 2028.” The PAC’s other Senate wins came in Republican strongholds, including Barry Moore in Alabama and Andy Barr in Kentucky.

Other spending failed to deliver the intended results, both in primaries and in November. The Fairshake network’s roughly $10 million spent opposing Juliana Stratton in the Illinois Democratic primary failed to block her nomination. She later won the Senate seat. In Massachusetts, John Deaton lost despite roughly $700,000 in spending by crypto-backed Commonwealth Unity Fund to help elect him. Ken Paxton, who received crypto PAC backing in his successful Texas Republican runoff against John Cornyn, went on to lose to James Talarico.

The House brought another setback when digital assets subcommittee chair Bryan Steil lost to Democrat Mitchell Berman despite $1.9 million in crypto PAC funding.

The shift in congressional control could slow crypto legislation, but BitGo CEO and co-founder Mike Belshe said the election results would not alter the industry’s trajectory. “Crypto has continued to move forward under different administrations and different political majorities. The GENIUS Act is now law, stablecoin rules are moving ahead, and regulators are continuing to work on tokenization and digital asset markets.”Democratic victories could also bring crypto supporters into the Senate. Advocacy group Stand With Crypto counted 19 winning Senate candidates it classified as pro-crypto and eight as anti-crypto, with 33 of the 35 Senate races called as of 9:45 p.m. UTC on Nov. 4.

Despite the crypto PAC spending against him in Texas, Talarico voted for the Texas Bitcoin reserve as a state lawmaker and is rated by the group as strongly supportive of crypto.

For industry advocates, the next challenge is building relationships with incoming lawmakers. Jessica Martinez, US policy director at Fireblocks, told Cointelegraph that many newly elected members had limited records on crypto, making “educating members early” key to shaping the Democratic policy agenda.

#5083. Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Why Bitcoin's Next Soft Fork Could Feel Like a Practice Run

Four flaws in Bitcoin’s consensus rules that have lingered for years.

Issues like quantum resistance may command attention, but a practical cleanup is what many experts think Bitcoin’s next soft fork is likely to address, spanning fixes for vulnerabilities in the network’s difficulty adjustment mechanism to denial-of-service vectors among miners.

Proposed in 2019 and known as the Great Consensus Cleanup (BIP-54), the upgrade seeks to address the “cruft” in Bitcoin’s consensus design through changes that have historically been sidelined, as described by long-time Bitcoin developer and BIP-54 co-author Matt Corallo.

“There’s a laundry list of things we know of that we really need to clean up,” he said during a recent appearance on MARA Foundation TV. “All of these issues are real concerns that could impact Bitcoin negatively, but they’re not like, ‘Oh, the sky is falling.’”

Although a formal timeline for the BIP’s undertaking hasn’t been established, Corallo thinks there’s an 80% chance that the Great Consensus Cleanup is up next for the network. During his talk with Head of MARA Foundation Isabel Foxen Duke, he also called it a practical exercise for Bitcoin developers who haven’t yet been through a soft fork.

At its core, Corallo said BIP-54 “fixes some stuff that everyone agrees are problems,” while targeting four specific vulnerabilities:

Still, not everyone views a maintenance-focused soft fork as a major win. On X, Paul Sztorc, co-founder and CEO of LayerTwo Labs, called BIP-54’s potential activation “kind of depressing, since it is a janitorial maintenance fork that doesn't do anything.”

Surveying developers at TABconf in Atlanta, Sztorc noted that Bitcoin’s “best and brightest” predominantly expect the Great Consensus Cleanup to be Bitcoin’s next soft fork anyway, with the earliest estimate in September 2027, and most pointing to 2028 or later.

Following the activation of Taproot in 2021, Bitcoin’s community has faced years of gridlock surrounding protocol changes. This friction is frequently framed around “ossification,” with broader protocol changes like OP_CAT (BIP-347) and OP_CHECKTEMPLATEVERIFY (BIP-119) struggling to reach ecosystem-wide consensus.

According to Corallo, soft forks have always involved overcoming technical hurdles. But more recently, he’s observed political and social friction that’s become just as relevant.

The Bitcoin community spent months debating how Taproot should be activated, with arguments pitting miner-signaling thresholds against mandatory user flag days. Corallo argues that BIP-54 can establish a deployment playbook for future soft forks.

“In every recent soft fork, there’s also been a bunch of drama about activation mechanisms,” he said. “So hopefully we could do a soft fork to go through that drama, and then copy and paste and reuse it and head off some of that drama [for future soft forks].”

In that sense, BIP-54 is just as much about proving Bitcoin can still agree on upgrades at the social layer as it is about actual code cleanup.

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.

#5079. BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

The crypto exchange’s founder warned of security staffing shortages amid restructuring as analysts flagged suspicious transfers potentially linked to the Mercado Bitcoin hack.

Cryptocurrency exchange BitMart is operating with an understaffed security team following restructuring, its founder said Wednesday, after confirming a new hack that may be linked to the recent attack on Brazil’s exchange Mercado Bitcoin.

In a post on X, BitMart founder Sheldon Xia said threats against employees and alleged retaliation by former staff had complicated efforts to retain technical personnel.

“It took considerable effort and individual conversations to persuade some key technical staff to remain and maintain our core systems,” Xia wrote. “However, we continue to face numerous attacks. Our security team is currently severely understaffed, but we are still doing everything we can to safeguard data and assets.”

Xia described the amount involved in Monday’s hack as small but did not disclose a figure. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery identified approximately $391,000 in suspicious transfers from wallets mostly associated with BitMart across three blockchains, according to a Wednesday investigation.

Bitquery traced the funds to a wallet also implicated in the more than $46 million Mercado Bitcoin hack on Monday, Nov. 2, supporting Xia’s preliminary suspicion of a possible connection between the incidents.

Related: North Korea denies role in $6.3B Binance hack, points finger at US

The latest breach comes amid months of uncertainty over BitMart’s future. The exchange announced in July that it would end trading on Aug. 26 and cease operations in January 2027. Following the announcement, users reported delayed withdrawals and frozen accounts, raising concerns about access to their funds.

In August, BitMart’s Chinese-language X account alleged that employees had gone unpaid and demanded answers about customer funds. The founder dismissed the allegations as fabricated, though it remained unclear who controlled the account at the time.

In a restructuring proposal published Monday, the exchange outlined three potential recovery options for users, including liquid asset distributions and recovery-linked tokens, with plans to seek court approval in December or January.

Cointelegraph contacted BitMart for details about the restructuring, security staffing and latest breach but had not received a response by publication.

Separately, a crypto crime-fighting alliance formed by Tron, Tether and TRM Labs has dissolved amid a wave of major industry hacks, Bloomberg reported Wednesday.

The T3 Financial Crime Unit, launched in September 2024 and later joined by Binance, struggled with slow responses and poor coordination, Bloomberg reported, citing people familiar with the matter. One source said the group disbanded in late August.

In May, T3 said it had helped freeze more than $450 million in assets linked to suspected criminal activity since its launch in 2024.

The group’s dissolution comes amid a wave of major crypto hacks, including last week’s $6.3 billion Binance breach, the largest cryptocurrency theft on record.

#5077. Mercado Bitcoin says phased reopening to begin Monday, will cover losses

Customers will initially be able to view their holdings, while withdrawals and trading remain suspended pending security checks.

Brazilian crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin said it will begin restoring customer account access Monday after a hack that blockchain analysts estimated resulted in more than $46 million in stolen assets.

In a statement to Cointelegraph, Mercado Bitcoin said customers would initially regain access through its website to view balances, statements, holdings and investments. Transfers, withdrawals and trading will resume in later phases, subject to independent security testing.

Mercado Bitcoin also pledged to use company funds to replenish assets affected by the breach, saying more than $15 million in stolen funds had already been frozen with help from the industry.

“Our trading desk is preparing to begin the necessary repurchases in domestic and international markets,” it said.

The update follows the exchange’s disclosure of a security breach on Monday, which saw attackers transferring funds from the company’s hot wallets and customer deposit addresses through standard transfers.

Addressing liquidity, Mercado said it had a strong cash position, bolstered by a $20 million investment from stablecoin issuer Tether to support operational expansion.

#5076. Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas Sues Kalshi, Joining a Growing List of States Targeting Prediction Markets

Arkansas has sued Kalshi, accusing the prediction market of offering an illegal, unregulated gambling platform to the state's residents, Attorney General Tim Griffin said on Wednesday.

The lawsuit, filed against KalshiEX LLC and Kalshi Inc., alleges multiple violations of the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act. The state is seeking restitution, penalties, taxes and an injunction.

Today I filed a lawsuit against @Kalshi for violating the Arkansas Deceptive Trade Practices Act by operating an unregulated gambling platform.

Kalshi has built a global business that it calls a prediction market, allowing consumers to wager money on the outcome of just about… pic.twitter.com/ePycNKXzy6

— Attorney General Tim Griffin (@AGTimGriffin) November 4, 2026

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said. "And gambling on an unregulated platform is squarely prohibited by Arkansas law."

Instead of obtaining licenses and paying the taxes required of gambling operators, Kalshi opened its platform to Arkansas residents as young as 18 and encouraged them to bet on sporting and entertainment events, Griffin alleged. "Arkansas law is concerned with substance, not semantics," he said.

Griffin pointed to Kalshi's own responsible-gambling tools, including self-exclusion, deposit limits and trading breaks, along with its partnership with the National Council on Problem Gambling, as evidence that the company knows its platform carries gambling-style risks. "Although Kalshi insists that its products are not gambling, its actions speak louder than its labels," he said.

Kalshi, which is regulated by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, has argued in other cases that its event contracts fall under exclusive federal jurisdiction and are not subject to state gambling laws.

Arkansas is the latest of more than a dozen states to take on prediction markets. Among the most significant cases:

Other states have also moved against prediction markets. Arizona filed criminal charges against Kalshi in March, a Washington court temporarily blocked its sports contracts in July, and Massachusetts' case is now before the state's highest court after a lower court barred Kalshi's sports contracts in January.

The CFTC has sued at least 10 states to defend what it says is its exclusive authority over prediction markets, and President Donald Trump has backed the agency, calling several state officials who challenged the platforms "scum."

Federal appeals courts are split. The Third Circuit ruled in April that New Jersey cannot regulate Kalshi's sports contracts, while the Sixth and Ninth Circuits sided with Ohio, Tennessee and Nevada. New Jersey has asked the Supreme Court to settle the question, backed by 39 other states and the District of Columbia, as well as the NFL. Kalshi's response to the petition is due November 9, and the Supreme Court is not expected to decide whether to take up the case until December at the earliest, ESPN reported.

#5073. India warns AI-powered hackers targeting crypto exchanges at ‘machine speed’

India’s cybersecurity agency says a threat actor is escalating an AI-assisted hacking campaign targeting crypto exchanges and other financial firms.

India’s national cybersecurity agency warned that an AI-assisted threat actor has escalated its attacks on cryptocurrency exchanges and other financial businesses, stealing wallet keys and siphoning funds.

The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) on Wednesday designated the threat actor as “CRAFTY FALCON,” in an update to a campaign it first warned about in October. The latest advisory has now explicitly identified crypto exchanges among affected businesses and detailed the use of AI attack tools.

“The threat is active and ongoing. CSIRT-Fin continues to receive reports of successful attacks, including from NBFCs and fintech entities that had already received the earlier restricted advisories and had undertaken remediation,” said CERT-In.

The warning adds to evidence of hackers using AI tools against financial institutions. In October, cybersecurity firm CrowdStrike reported that an unidentified attacker used ARTEX, an AI penetration-testing tool also named in CERT-In’s advisory, alongside Claude Code and other AI models to breach South Korean financial firms and steal data.

CERT-In said the AI-assisted financial threat actor “surveys its targets with machine intelligence, operates at machine speed and strikes by invoking the victim’s own payment and disbursement APIs.”

The campaign has targeted nonbank lenders, digital lending platforms, fintech companies, payment aggregators and service providers, as well as crypto exchanges.

According to CERT-In, attackers exploited vulnerable applications and exposed testing environments, using shared credentials and access tokens to move into production systems. They also targeted cloud infrastructure to deploy malicious files.

AI tools helped attackers rapidly generate and refine attack scripts. The attackers also reverse-engineered application code to develop tailored exploits, while some attacks were timed for weekends and holidays to delay detection.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

In some incidents, attackers used small test transactions to validate unauthorized transfers before moving larger sums. CERT-In also reported stolen cryptocurrency wallet keys and funds drained from wallets.

The agency did not name any affected crypto exchanges or quantify losses.

Cointelegraph reached out to CERT-In, CoinDCX, CoinSwitch, WazirX, ZebPay and Mudrex for comment.

CERT-In has advised entities to “treat this campaign as active and escalating,” urging at-risk companies to heighten security monitoring around the clock, with increased staffing and lowered alert thresholds during holidays, weekends and other low-coverage windows.

Solana Foundation chief information security officer Michael Coates previously told Cointelegraph that legitimate security teams need access to the strongest AI tools.

“We need to make sure that the best models we can get are in the hands of defenders because attackers will have something capable enough,” he said.

TRM Labs head of policy Ari Redbord similarly told Cointelegraph in July that “attack techniques are moving faster than a single audit from launch day can account for,” urging continuous security reviews.

#5068. It Just Got Easier to Get Peptides in the US. Here's What Could Come Next.

Spoiler: More access doesn’t necessarily mean more safety.

ON WEDNESDAY, THE U.S. Food and Drug Administration announced a new policy that makes six experimental peptides more readily available. According to a document released by the HHS, the government will not penalize specialized pharmacies for making and supplying these popular substances to patients with a prescription. The peptides: BPC-157, epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500.

This does not mean these particular peptides have been approved for use by the FDA. In fact, none of them have gone through the rigorous human testing required for FDA drug approval. And the new policy is not intended to be permanent. Instead, it allows temporary access as the FDA decides on a longer-term plan for the six peptides. It also includes the caveat that compounders report “serious adverse events, and product quality issues to FDA and to the States where the products are compounded,” according to the document.

This topic has been red-hot since July, when the FDA held an advisory committee meeting to consider whether to add these peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply. These substances are already widely used and obtained on the gray market. If people are going to use them, advocates have argued, it would be safest if those substances came from licensed pharmacies and are used with physician oversight.

Critics, on the other hand—including FDA scientists—are concerned that the government may be opening the floodgates to under-studied and potentially harmful drugs. These substances, they note, have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness. And some of these peptides are already being promoted and sold on the basis of scientific claims that experts say are unsupported or questionable.

Here's what could come next, if you're thinking about taking one of these six compounded peptides.

Some parts of the current conversation played out at last month’s PepMed conference in Washington, D.C. A sign pointing guests to the registration table featured a stylized gold peptide overlaid on the white dome of the U.S. Capitol. PepMed’s organizer, the American Academy of Peptide Medicine (AAPM), founded just last year, has already become a major voice in this debate.

In advance of the FDA’s July meeting, AAPM submitted hundreds of pages to a crowded public docket, including information about each of the seven peptides then under review. Several of its advisory board members spoke during the meeting’s public comment sessions. And the group appears to have the ear of the administration. PepMed speakers included the assistant secretary for health, Admiral Brian Christine, and a host of prominent MAHA figures.

The September event opened with an on-the-record media hour, featuring a dozen speakers, including physicians and owners and CEOs of compounding pharmacies and telehealth companies. A recurring theme was the danger of the gray market, where peptide quality can be difficult or impossible to verify and consumers often make treatment decisions without medical oversight—“this is not a victory for patient safety,” said Ricardo Rossello, PhD, a U.S. congressional delegate for Puerto Rico and AAPM advisory board member who co-founded Beijing Prosperous Biopharm, a medical research company.

Many of the speakers also lamented the failure of the U.S. health care system to fully meet the needs of patients, and some of the day’s speakers, including Alex Tatem, MD, an Indiana-based urologist and men’s health specialist with a large YouTube following, presented experimental peptides as part of the solution. “Our established medical system is very much a sick care model,” said Tatem, who serves on the AAPM’s medical advisory board. It’s important to help patients in a health crisis, he told me in an interview. But people want to be proactive about disease prevention. In his view, the peptides under FDA’s consideration can play a role by expanding “the toolkit that’s available to providers.”

At the FDA’s July advisory committee meeting, the agency’s scientists advised against allowing broad access to these peptides, in part because they have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness in the kind of long-term, rigorously designed human studies typically required for drug approval.Even at the conference, some doctors acknowledged the evidence gaps. Abud Bakri, MD, works in California as a physician who specializes in caring for hospitalized patients. He also runs a separate medical practice focused on preventive and longevity medicine, and he serves on the Enhanced Games Independent Medical Commission. His interest in peptides has been long-standing enough to predate his medical career.

Bakri, now 33, has been a powerlifter since he was 17. He explained that he has a genetic condition that may increase his risk for ligament injuries. He first used BPC-157 almost a decade ago, and believes the peptide helped him recover faster, though he acknowledges that this is hard to prove definitively, given the absence of well-designed human studies.

If just a fraction of the rat and Petri dish research holds up in humans, BPC-157 could be an incredibly helpful drug in the ICU, he told me at the conference, during an interview at an outdoor dining area. But ideally, he says, its effectiveness should be proven before it’s used in this setting and potential side effects should be better understood. “If I’m going to have an honest conversation with a patient,” he said, “I need to be able to fully elucidate the risk, which I can’t for some of these compounds.”

After the conference, I spoke by Zoom with Thomas Kremen, MD, a physician at UCLA Health who works for several athletic teams, including the Los Angeles Chargers and Lakers. Kremen and his colleagues recently reviewed the evidence behind six popular experimental peptides. He and his co-authors seemed unimpressed. “It’s probably not a strong stance to be recommending these things,” he told me.

Would it be accurate for physicians to tell their patients that a peptide like BPC-157 heals tissue?

“Maybe if they're talking about a rat in some study,” he said. “But I think if you're making a claim, you better be able to cite the literature that supports that.”

Right now, not only is there a dearth of high-quality clinical evidence that peptides work and are safe, unsupported claims are being made about these compounds.

For instance, among the documents that AAPM submitted to the FDA’s public docket was a white paper claiming that two experimental peptides—epitalon and BPC-157—are endogenous, indicating they are naturally produced by the body. Similar descriptions can be found on websites for telehealth companies and online peptide vendors suggesting their products are synthetic versions of naturally derived peptides.

Epitalon is a lab-made peptide based on an extract that researchers in the Soviet Union obtained from cattle brains. Terry Roberts, PhD, a researcher who studies epitalon at Brunel University of London, said it’s unclear whether humans produce this peptide. As far as he’s aware, no studies have explicitly described its presence in humans.

And BPC-157? The peptide was discovered in gastric juice, said Vince Luca, PhD, a researcher at the Moffitt Cancer Center in Florida. Yet 35 years after its discovery, nobody has found a gene that encodes for it—it could have come from a microbe or from food. It’s not impossible that the body produces BPC-157, said Luca, but he thinks it’s unlikely. He also says that health care professionals “should know enough not to tell [people] that it's an endogenous peptide” when it is very unlikely to be one. And regardless, he continued, “there are plenty of endogenous peptides that, injected at high concentrations, would have a really negative effect on the body.” In other words, being endogenous alone doesn’t mean it’s safe.

(The American Academy of Peptide Medicine’s press team did not respond to queries asking how the group determined that BPC-157 and epitalon are endogenous.)

“My prediction is that those who are already using the gray market will continue to do so,” said Ryan Beattie, director of business development at Adaptog Research & Life Sciences, a UK-based company that sells experimental peptides. “So I think in the initial term, people will use compounding pharmacies in the US. What I then predict will happen is people will then get used to it, get more aware of it—and then switch to the gray market because of the cost.”

He may be right. For this story, we spoke with two peptide users who had been living with chronic pain, and after trying physical therapy as well as other treatment approaches, turned to the Wolverine stack, a combination of BPC-157 and TB-500. Both said they felt the peptides helped them—and neither was sure they’d switch to compounded versions, which are more expensive than the gray market and aren’t covered by insurance. One of them, Charles A., asked, “What are the hoops and what are the costs going to be?”

A third person, who asked to be referred to as James—the name he uses in online communities—tried an oral version of BPC-157 four years ago and said he hasn’t been the same since then. Shortly after taking the experimental compound, he felt anxiety followed by sensory and emotional numbness that still hasn’t gone away. He also developed an autoimmune condition. Online, he found others with some similar experiences. When he looked carefully at the published research, he concluded that it didn’t match the online hype. “I felt very foolish afterwards.”

Some scientists are continuing to investigate these peptides. At Moffitt, Luca is currently studying BPC-157 and he’s keeping an open mind.

Over the next few months, he and his colleagues plan to run a variety of very basic experiments in which they add the peptide to human cells and see how those cells respond. The results could provide additional insight into what, precisely, this molecule is doing in the body—and they may help explain why users have reported everything from striking benefits to lasting harm.

Perhaps one day, BPC-157 will prove to be an effective drug, said Luca. But for now, his advice is to wait on using it and other experimental peptides: “I just worry that people are a little too cavalier in how these are handled.”

Sara Talpos is a Michigan-based journalist covering biomedical research and public health. She’s written for publications including STAT, Science, and Undark, where she’s a contributing editor.

#5067. Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

Canadian Man Extradited to US Over Plot to Hijack University Supercomputers for Crypto

A Canadian man has been extradited to the United States to face charges that he helped hack into university computers to steal login credentials and secretly mine cryptocurrency, the Justice Department said on Tuesday.

A federal grand jury in the Western District of New York indicted Ryan James Roach, 35, of Saskatchewan, and Mathiew James Stubbings, 41, of Ontario, on charges of conspiracy to obtain information from and damage a protected computer, obtaining information from a protected computer, and intentionally damaging a protected computer. The charges carry a maximum sentence of 10 years in prison and a $250,000 fine.

Canadian man extradited to the U.S. for his role in computer hacking scheme targeting universities... @FBIBuffalo @USDOJ_Intl https://t.co/vY8IacXdZZ pic.twitter.com/NsSZ5BIkIf

Between June 2016 and December 2019, the pair targeted U.S. educational institutions with supercomputers, researching security weaknesses in their networks before breaking in, according to the indictment. Once inside, they allegedly deployed malware, stole login credentials and forced the victims' computers to mine cryptocurrency for their own benefit.

Victims included institutions in New York, Tennessee, North Carolina and Massachusetts, among them a university in the Western District of New York, prosecutors said. The pair allegedly used virtual private servers in several countries, registered under false names, to send malware and mining software to victim computers and store stolen credentials, and routed attacks through computers compromised at other victims to hide their identities.

Roach was extradited from Canada on October 28 and arraigned in Rochester, New York, where he was held pending a detention hearing. Extradition proceedings against Stubbings are continuing in Canada.

The scheme is a form of cryptojacking, in which attackers use other people's computers to mine crypto while the victims bear the electricity costs and slower performance. The Justice Department did not say which cryptocurrency the pair allegedly mined.

But hackers looking to profit from stolen computing power may be turning elsewhere. In a report published Monday, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said it expects attackers to "increasingly prioritize LLMJacking over traditional resource theft like cryptojacking," hijacking companies' access to AI models and passing on the bills.

Unlike the gradual drain of cryptojacking, LLMjacking can run up huge cloud charges within hours, Google said. In incidents investigated by its Mandiant unit this year, fraudulent charges topped $100,000 in a single day, and some organizations racked up $1 million before access was cut off. Attackers are reselling stolen access through underground services that Google calls "LLM Mixers."

#5066. ‘Lawyer up’: Democrats target Trump, Big Tech after midterm sweep

Democrats wasted little time after their midterm victory, warning tech CEOs to prepare for subpoenas and signaling fresh investigations into Trump’s business dealings.

Democratic lawmakers said Big Tech executives should expect a wave of subpoenas, along with investigations into US President Donald Trump’s business circle when the new Congress takes office in January.

As Democrats secured control of Congress in a sweeping victory on Tuesday, Democratic lawmakers were already firing warning shots at the Trump family and tech executives close to the US president.

“They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we’ll be printing,” said US Senator Ed Markey, who sits on the Senate Commerce Committee, referring to Big Tech executives.

US Representative Robert Garcia, the expected incoming House Oversight Committee chairman, said he plans to take on “the Trump Crime Family, corporate corruption, DHS and ICE abuses, and the cover-up of the Epstein files,” adding a two-word warning: “Lawyer up.”

The warnings follow a mid-term election campaign in which Democrats challenged Big Tech’s growing political influence and the rapid expansion of AI, raising concerns about data centers’ demands on electricity, safeguards for AI products and tech executives’ ties to Trump.

Punchbowl News reported on Wednesday that technology executives close to Trump, including Elon Musk, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, were expected to face scrutiny from House Oversight, Energy and Commerce and Senate Commerce.

The outlet also identified OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei as potential witnesses facing questions about their companies’ AI products.

Trump’s crypto dealings also featured in mid-term Democratic campaign attacks, with candidates including Virginia Senator Mark Warner highlighting his memecoin earnings as part of a broader pattern of alleged corruption and self-enrichment.

“The amount of grift and corruption is unprecedented in American politics,” said Garcia during a press conference on Wednesday.

“The president himself has a crypto scheme where he’s making billions of dollars. This family is billions richer as the American public struggles.”

#5064. Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance has removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus, a third-party crypto trading platform, after SignalPlus reported a "potential security issue," the exchange said on Thursday.

"Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe," the exchange said, describing the move as an "immediate precautionary measure."

Following a potential security issue reported by third-party platform SignalPlus, we have proactively removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus as an immediate precautionary measure.

Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe.

If you used SignalPlus, please… pic.twitter.com/0HYYkTAW2p

API keys let third-party apps and trading tools connect to a user's exchange account and place trades on their behalf. Removing them cuts off that connection, meaning any automated strategies running on Binance through SignalPlus will stop until users set up new keys.

Binance urged SignalPlus users to log into their accounts directly to review their positions and open orders. It recommended that they wait until SignalPlus confirms the issue is fully resolved before reconnecting, and that they always enable IP whitelisting, which restricts an API key to approved internet addresses.

The exchange also called on all third-party platforms integrated with Binance to review their security controls, watch for unusual activity and contact it immediately if they spot any concerns.

SignalPlus, a Hong Kong-based platform that provides trading tools for crypto options and derivatives, connects to several exchanges, including Binance.

The incident comes a week after Binance was hit by the largest hack in crypto history, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from its hot wallets on October 29. The FBI has attributed the theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor, a claim Pyongyang has denied.

Binance has said the attack began when a fake recruiter persuaded one of its engineers to download a malware-laced coding assignment, and that the attackers then used stolen credentials to reach an internal wallet signing service.

Binance said on Wednesday that it has replenished about 98% of the stolen assets from its own funds, and is still buying the rest, mostly less liquid tokens, through market and over-the-counter channels. It has also taken a new proof-of-reserves snapshot covering 44 tokens, up from 10, which users will be able to verify once its zero-knowledge proof is generated in about 50 to 60 hours.

The exchange is still restoring services. On Thursday, it began reopening withdrawals for more assets in phases, prioritizing tokens based on user demand over the past week. Deposits remain open.

#5058. Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Samsung is putting digital dollars in the same app where its users keep their credit cards and boarding passes.

Stablecoin accounts went live in Samsung Wallet on Thursday for eligible U.S. Galaxy users, letting them buy, hold, send, and receive Circle’s USDC on the Solana blockchain without downloading a separate crypto app or managing private keys. The feature reaches about 82 million compatible Galaxy devices in the U.S., according to the announcement.

“For a decade, stablecoins have proved the technology works,” said Lily Liu, president of the Solana Foundation. “What they lacked was a way to reach the mainstream.”

Samsung first announced the feature on Oct. 7, when the electronics giant tapped Solana for USDC transfers. Samsung Wallet first teased plans to support the stablecoin, which is pegged to the U.S. dollar, over the summer.

The product is built with Bastion, a licensed stablecoin custodian and infrastructure provider, and Coinbase, which safeguards the USDC as Bastion’s sub-custodian through its Coinbase Prime Vault. Samsung’s disclosures stress that the account is a crypto wallet, not a bank account.

Users can send USDC to other Samsung Wallet users in the U.S. and to compatible outside crypto wallets with no Samsung transfer fee, though other providers may charge one. They can also send money to qualifying bank accounts in more than 60 countries, where recipients may receive local currency, with fees that vary by destination.

Topping up with a credit card, including through Samsung Pay, carries a 4% fee, and withdrawals to a bank account or debit card also come with a fee. New users who make a first top-up of $50 or more can receive $25 in USDC. The service requires Android 13 or later and is open to U.S. residents 18 and older, except those in New York.

"Stablecoins have the potential to make moving money around the world faster and easier, and we want Galaxy users to be able to take advantage of that without having to navigate the complexity of traditional crypto tools,” said Woncheol Chai, who leads the digital wallet team in Samsung’s mobile business. “Solana helps us bring that experience to Samsung's scale."

Samsung said it expects to explore online and in-store stablecoin payments and to expand to additional markets, subject to local regulation.

#5055. Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🗳️ Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Market Structure Bill Resurfaces

Bitcoin spent Tuesday night under $76,000, a seven-week low, with traders bracing for whatever a Democratic Congress meant for crypto. By Wednesday afternoon it was back above $80,800, up 4% on the day.

The easy story is that uncertainty lifted and crypto exhaled. And that could be part of it. But oil helped. WTI fell 9% to $88 a barrel, down almost 17% on the week, as Iran’s parliament speaker said voters had “said NO TO WAR.” The DOW jumped 3% on the day and Nasdaq +1.6% in what was a huge green day across markets.

James Butterfill of CoinShares put it plainly: “For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections.”

But the policy news that landed Wednesday afternoon matters more than the candles.

Eleanor Terrett reported that House Democrats are discussing expanding Maxine Waters’ Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, originally an amendment to the CLARITY Act in 2025, into a full market structure bill next Congress. The draft would fold in Democratic CLARITY provisions that the House rejected last year, plus provisions Senate Democrats and Republicans had already agreed on before the September cloture vote that killed the bill. Three industry sources described the talks to her.

That’s a genuine surprise. Twenty-four hours ago the consensus was that market structure legislation was dead for two years. Waters is the likely incoming chair of House Financial Services, and she is apparently planning to write a bill rather than hold hearings. But the bill will have some changes.

The new proposals under discussion would give the SEC a larger role supervising crypto markets and leave the CFTC with a smaller one. The draft is expected to carry ethics provisions restricting the crypto activities of Trump and other federal officials. Those provisions are what Democrats wanted all along, and what many thought would kill any chances the bill has of getting signed.

So one of the first courses of action from this Democrat-led Congress is to enact some reasonable changes to the Clarity Act to try to get it passed in 2027. That feels like a very bullish first step.

#5054. Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Polygon block producers have already frozen the stolen tokens, but a proposed hard fork would allow the funds to be recovered and returned to their owners.

Binance and Polymarket have proposed a Polygon network hard fork to recover approximately 70.49 million POL tokens, worth about $6.8 million, stolen in separate security incidents involving the two platforms.

According to a Wednesday governance proposal, the recovery would cover 23.86 million POL, worth $2.31 million across four addresses associated with the Polymarket hack and 46.63 million POL, worth about $4.51 million, held in one address linked to the Binance hack.

The proposed hard fork would remove the stolen tokens from the five attacker-controlled addresses and transfer them to verified recovery wallets designated by Polymarket and Binance. The process would take place at a predetermined block without changing POL’s total supply.

Polygon block producers have already blocked transactions from the affected addresses, preventing the stolen funds from moving. However, the proposal said the restrictions are temporary measures that cannot return the funds to their owners.

The plan requires governance approval and testing on Polygon’s Amoy testnet before activation. It is scheduled for discussion on Nov. 5, with no confirmed date for the mainnet hard fork.

The proposal follows Binance’s record $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29 and a separate incident in which Polymarket temporarily suspended trading after reporting that a third-party provider had been compromised.

#5053. Do Your Muscles Need a ‘Creatine Washout?’ It Depends.

The benefits might be more mental than physical.

CREATINE IS THE prom king of social media—everyone’s talking about it. And not always nice things, either. Because for all the (very warranted) complements about how creatine can help you grow stronger, there’s also some (baseless) behind-the-back whispers about how it can make your hair fall out, poison you with heavy metals, and yes even kill you.

Between this polarization, there’s the seemingly reasonable personal stance of Andrew Huberman, PhD: Take creatine, but also take a creatine break.

Just in case you’re unfamiliar, a Huberman is a neuroscientist and associate professor in the Departments of Neurobiology and Ophthalmology at Stanford University School of Medicine. And he only hosts one of the most popular health podcasts of all time, Huberman Lab. When Huberman says anything about anything, people listen.

So when Huberman posted that he does a “creatine washout” every four months “just to see what effect it continues to have over time,” you have to wonder.

IT’S THE TIME period after you stop supplementing with creatine. The term is used frequently in scientific research that assesses the effects of creatine supplementation, often in athletic performance studies.

But, admittedly, there aren’t that many studies that look at the creatine washout phase itself. The most recent goes all the way back to 2004: “Effects of repeated creatine supplementation on muscle, plasma, and urine creative levels.”

In that study researchers found that levels of muscle phosphocreatine—that’s the amount of creatine stored within muscle cells—did not return to pre-supplementation baselines even after a 30-day washout period.

Since that 2004 study, there hasn’t been any supportive—or contradictory—science, but the lead author says the findings hold up more than 20 years later.

“Four to six weeks is a typical response and depends on dietary creatine, response to supplement, etc.,” says Eric Rawson, PhD, FACSM, Chair of the Department of Health, Nutrition, and Exercise Science at Messiah University, and a co-author of the 2004 washout study.

MAYBE, BUT NOT for reasons you might think.

“Questions about creatine washout and/or cycling supplementation are best handled from both physiological and behavioral perspectives,” says Rawson. “When I am asked about washout and cycling, I always ask people if they have similar concerns about their multivitamin. Or their calcium supplements. Or whey protein.”

Meaning, do you have the same washout concerns about creatine as you do your other supplements? Probably not.

“There appears to be a lingering concern about long-term creatine supplementation that is not supported by data.”

That said, Rawson says that some people, including many athletes, can feel overwhelmed by demands of maintaining their fitness edge—to the point where it’s good to take a break for mental health.

“Taking an off-season break from some of the usual routines, which includes, training, meal frequency, macro-nutrient intake goals, and the daily dietary supplement routine, can provide a break and perhaps some relief from the usual routine,” he says.

How long a break is sort of up to you and your long-term goals. But let your body and brain be the judge of that—not whatever you hear on social.

Paul Kita is a Deputy Editor at Men's Health, where he has covered food, cooking, nutrition, supplements, grooming, tech, travel, and fatherhood at the brand for more than 15 years. He is also the author of two Men's Health cookbooks, Guy Gourmet and A Man, A Pan, A Plan, and the winner of a James Beard Award.

#5052. The 6 Best Dermaplaning Tools to Use at Home, Tested by Editors and Dermatologists

Dermaflash's Luxe Plus Device came out on top in our testing.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Dermaplaning is the biggest game changer in my beauty routine, which is saying a lot as a beauty editor who has tried every facial hair removal method. It's an exfoliating procedure that uses a blade to scrape the skin's surface to remove dead skin cells, dirt, debris, and peach fuzz, says dermatologist Azadeh Shirazi, MD. "When your skin is hairless and buildup-free, it looks super toned and bright." Plus, a freshly shaved face allows my serums and moisturizers to absorb deeper, and my makeup to glide on easier. Dermaplaning is my tool of choice for facial hair removal.

And here's the thing: You could splurge on a pricey salon service with a trained aesthetician, but I'm all about the DIY approach. While a professional treatment is more precise, at-home dermaplaning is cheaper, faster, and more convenient, especially since I have a busy schedule.

My guide is a good place to start your shopping, but you might want to also keep these pointers in mind if you're in the market for a new dermaplaning tool—or you're finally ready to try one for the first time.

Dr. Shirazi says the most important factor to consider is a blade that is made from a durable, high-quality material. The best devices I've tested feature a stainless steel blade. That's because Dr. Shirazi says it's easy to clean, is resistant to rust, and is super sharp.

When dermaplaning I prefer devices with a grippy handle, which helps control my strokes for a precise shave. More specifically, look for a tool that has a weighted handle, Dr. Shirazi says. "It should be long and have a good grip as you make the strokes on the skin." Some devices have rubber grippers for extra grip.

At-home skincare tools have become increasingly popular, and there are so many options to choose from, so I took matters into my own hands to carefully research the most popular and top-rated dermaplaning tools. After narrowing my choices to 12 picks, I tested these contenders before finalizing my list. I examined the safety features, blade materials, effectiveness, and comfort to ensure these are truly the best dermaplaning tools in the game. I spent several months testing these devices to ensure they're totally worth your hard-earned money.

And to ensure that there were a slew of options across all budgets, I tapped WH contributor Lily Wohlner, two dermatologists, and an aesthetician for their guidance. Ultimately, we only liked these six dermaplaning tools. We think you'll like them too.

Dermaflash earned major snaps from me, Wohlner, and Dr. Camp, so I knew it was our clear best overall pick. This high-tech gadget has a gentle pulsing technology and a super sharp blade to remove peach fuzz and dead skin cells in one fell swoop.

It provides an in-spa experience from the comfort of my bathroom. I'm always *shook* at how super smooth my face looks and feels after using the Dermaflash. The combo of tiny corrugations along the blade and gentle pulsing technology removes peach fuzz and dead skin cells swiftly without any nicks. The entire treatment is just a few minutes, making it a wonderful treat for busy women like myself.

The exfoliation leaves my skin more luminous before applying face moisturizer. Plus, my liquid, cream, and powder makeup applies easily, and caking is non-existent since using this device. My serums and moisturizer also sink in so quickly. I see the best results when I use it every two weeks, but honestly, I use it closer to every month, and it feels like such a treat.

Yes, this is by far the most expensive option on the list, but it's worth the splurge because it provides such a close shave in seconds. The Dermaflash feels so sturdy and comfortable to use in my hand that even relative dermaplaning beginners like myself can feel confident removing peach fuzz. The sharp blade ensures I get a close shave without scratching my sensitive skin, and the thick handle makes it easier to control.

I've found that applying a gentle oil to my skin before using these blades works wonders. It creates a smooth glide that transforms a regular at-home dermaplaning session into a luxurious spa-like experience.

This device has two options: a precision blade for targeting tricky spots like the upper lip, and a larger blade for covering the forehead and cheeks. Each tool comes with a single large blade, and the precise model is purchased separately.

I love how easy it is to switch between the two blades. Just pop on the clear plastic safety cover over the blade, slide the current head off, then slide the replacement blade with its new lid on and you're good to go.

The compact precision head has a smooth blade that quickly grooms the brows, upper lip, and precise areas of the chin where little hairs might pop up.

Regardless of which blade I opt for, I found they both provide a super close shave. Stacked glides easily across my face and eased my fear of accidentally cutting myself. And Dr. Camp backs me up, saying Stacked can improve the skin's tone and texture with consistent use.

If you don't want to spend a lot of money on an electric dermaplaning tool, opt for this suuuper affordable one from Spa Sciences. It uses sonic technology and a disposable blade to instantly remove peach fuzz, buildup, and dead skin. While each treatment lasts a month, she likes to incorporate this device into her skincare routine once a week to keep her face consistently smooth and clear.

Versed is Dr. Shirazi's no-fail recommendation, and it's popular among WH readers. Sadly, Versed is discontinuing this beloved tool, but they still have some in stock that you can buy immediately. Need two more reasons to shop right now? They're discounted by $10 when you order directly from Versed. Only 12 are available on Amazon, so once they're gone, they're gone forever.

So what makes them so great? While it can be tricky to dermaplane yourself, Dr. Shirazi credits the weighted round handle with providing ample control during each sesh. Despite the relatively affordable cost, it's made with high-quality materials, including the single edge, stainless steel blades that give the closest exfoliation treatment possible, she says.

But what thousands of Amazon shoppers appreciate most is that it works for both the brows and face. Plus, the vibrant shades also make the tool easy to spot in your makeup bag. No wonder Schick is the No. 1 best seller on Amazon. It’s affordable and effective, and it has amassed 152,600 perfect 5-star Amazon reviews.

Truly, the only downside is mastering your dermaplaning technique. Some reviewers accidentally nicked their face by applying too much pressure. To avoid this, pull your face taut, slowly glide the blade across the skin, and don’t shave over the same spot twice.

Doing treatments at home (versus in-office) and the results they bring are slightly trickier to predict and come with more risks, especially the chance of infections, scarring, or skin damage, cautions Dr. Shirazi. Her advice is to take it slow and try it only if you're comfortable. It's important to thoroughly cleanse the area you'll be treating, clean your hands, sanitize the tools, and tidy up the space, she says. "Be informed about dermaplaning—the more you know, the better you can assess the risks."

Keep in mind that dermaplaning might not suit those with sensitive or acne-prone skin. "You need a strong skin barrier to tolerate the treatment, so if you have dry skin or flaking red skin, it’s best to let the skin heal before dermaplaning," she says. "It can also worsen certain skin conditions like acne, psoriasis, eczema, and hives."

The number one no-no when it comes to dermaplaning: Don't over-exfoliate. "If you are on retinoids or exfoliants like glycolic or salicylic acid, you need to stop them a week before," says Dr. Shirazi. She also warns against dermaplaning if you have active wounds or skin conditions such as eczema and active wounds, as your skin needs to heal fully before dermaplaning. Lastly, don’t forget to thoroughly cleanse both the skin and hands before using the tool.

If you don't know how to use an at-home dermaplaning device, follow these simple steps with your manual or electric tool: Place the device at a 90-degree angle on the skin, starting at the top of the cheekbone near the hairline. Hold the skin taut as you gently glide the tool toward your nose. Continue until you have covered the entire cheek and jaw area. Repeat these steps across the forehead, lip, and chin.

Dermaplaning can be performed once every four to eight weeks, according to Dr. Shirazi. However, some brands recommend using their specific tools more often. Make sure to read the label on your device or consult your dermatologist if you're unsure how often to use your tool.

Her advice is to take it slow and try it only if you're comfortable. "Be informed about dermaplaning—the more you know, the better you can assess the risks."

#5051. Crédit Mutuel Arkéa resumes operations after attack, assesses losses

The bank said customer funds were unaffected after attackers accessed its systems through a service provider’s platform.

French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa said nearly all operations resumed Wednesday after a cyberattack linked to a suspected theft from the bank’s own account.

The bank said operations resumed following security checks and additional safeguards. It said customer funds were unaffected and the disruption only delayed certain transfers.

The bank also disclosed that attackers gained access through a service provider, not its online banking subsidiary Fortuneo. They transferred the bank’s money to other financial institutions, potentially allowing them to convert it into crypto.

Withdrawals from crypto exchanges first exposed the suspected theft. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery traced withdrawals from accounts at Kraken, Crypto.com and OKX before the victim was publicly identified.

Onchain sleuth ZachXBT flagged more than $106 million in suspicious withdrawals on Monday and raised his estimate above $133 million on Tuesday after finding another $27 million. His Wednesday report identified Arkéa as the potential victim.

The bank then confirmed the attack and said it was still assessing its financial impact.

“We are not confirming these figures; we can only confirm the approximate scale,” Arkéa said, according to a machine translation.

Arkéa said some funds had been frozen and further recovery efforts were underway. Kraken and Crypto.com previously told Cointelegraph they had restricted accounts or blocked funds linked to the suspected theft. Both exchanges maintained that their platforms remained secure.

#5048. Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman has hit back at criticism of his comments about computer memory, saying they were taken out of context and that he has long argued for the world to make more of it.

"I actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context," Altman tweeted early Wednesday. "I have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time."

but only because...i actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context.

i have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time.

The post came as Altman leaned into the backlash. Replying to users joking about his remarks, he said he "lol'd" at one meme and, when another asked him to rename his X account "Ram Altman," replied "ok yeah," briefly changing his display name before explaining he had only done so because he wanted people to have plenty of memory.

Altman's original comments came in an interview with YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, where he discussed shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom. "From like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time," he said. "You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment."

In the same interview, Altman also blamed memory makers for being slow to expand production, and said he would be "very sad" if prices for chips and other components did not fall.

The remarks drew widespread criticism, including from Elon Musk, who mocked Altman on Sunday with a meme of the Inglourious Basterds character Hans Landa asking, "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote.

The spat comes amid a global memory shortage driven by demand from AI data centers. SK hynix, one of the world's largest memory makers, warned last week that demand for memory would outstrip supply through 2030.

Contract prices for 16GB DDR5 memory chips rose from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 in September 2026, an increase of more than 550%, according to Tom's Hardware. On an earnings call this week, Chen Ching-wen, CEO of memory maker Team Group, said there was "no downward price trend whatsoever," and warned that some buyers could face a situation where "even money can't buy goods."

The crunch has already pushed up prices for consumer devices. In April, Meta raised the price of its Quest 3 and Quest 3S headsets by $50 to $100, weeks after Sony increased PlayStation 5 prices.

#5045. Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Plunge, $428 Million in Crypto Longs Get Wiped Out

Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Plunge, $428 Million in Crypto Longs Get Wiped Out

Bitcoin erased Wednesday's post-election rebound Thursday as a technology stock selloff spread to cryptocurrencies, triggering hundreds of millions in forced liquidations.

Bitcoin fell to about $76,500, down nearly 5% and back near levels seen before Wednesday's jump above $80,000. Ethereum shed more than 5%, while other major tokens suffered comparable losses. Roughly 90% of the top 100 cryptocurrencies were in the red during the selloff, while roughly $428 million in crypto longs were liquidated in one hour.

Wall Street offered no shelter. The S&P 500 lost about 2.6% in late-morning trading, while the Nasdaq sank nearly 4%, approaching a correction, or a 10% drop from its peak.

The election is part of the answer, but anxiety over AI spending and interest rates is making the damage worse.

Democrats' sweep of Congress initially rattled investors expecting tougher oversight of industries favored by President Donald Trump. Stocks rallied Wednesday as traders bet gridlock would limit major policy changes and falling oil prices would ease inflation. Bitwise Europe analyst André Dragosch also told Decrypt the Democratic sweep was "largely priced in."

Thursday brought a different interpretation. Investors weighed what Democratic control might mean for the data-center boom, particularly in states where voters oppose rising electricity costs and new facilities.

And the new political configuration is already moving things. Voters in at least 29 U.S. jurisdictions backed restrictions on data-center development, although not every measure is binding. UBS estimates potential pauses could threaten 30% of planned U.S. data-center capacity additions in 2028, per Reuters.

"We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout," Swissquote senior analyst Ipek Ozkardeskaya told Reuters.

Chipmakers and AI infrastructure companies took the hit first. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index dropped about 8%, while CoreWeave and Nebius fell by double digits. Tesla also tumbled roughly 9.5%, extending losses after Elon Musk spent heavily backing Republican candidates. Investors were also questioning the returns on massive AI investments.

The S&P 500 had the biggest bearish intraday movement in months, dropping almost 4% in minutes.

"The market's moving from the promise of AI to the price of AI," Ted Parkhill, chief executive of Incline Investment Management, told Reuters.

The political shift also threatens crypto's ambitions in Washington. Democratic control complicates efforts to revive the stalled Clarity Act, a proposed federal rulebook for digital assets. The new Congress will not take office until January. Politics alone cannot explain Bitcoin losing thousands of dollars in an hour.

Wednesday's rally looked dramatic, but much of its buying pressure came from traders closing losing bets against Bitcoin. About $378 million in short positions were liquidated in one hour as Bitcoin pushed above $80,000, according to CoinGlass data.

Today’s reversal flipped the liquidation machinery in the opposite direction. Exchanges automatically close underfunded longs as prices fall, generating more selling and the enormous red candles visible on Bitcoin's hourly chart.

Crypto was already vulnerable before the election. The market had lost roughly $300 billion in value over the previous month, amid concerns about AI spending, rising borrowing costs and the fallout from last week's multibillion-dollar Binance hack.

Traders also expect another Federal Reserve rate increase in December as inflation persists. Friday's October jobs report could alter those bets; higher rates generally hurt demand for speculative investments.

Technically, Bitcoin's still-forming daily candle is threatening a bearish engulfing pattern, potentially swallowing Wednesday's green body and erasing the breakout in a single session.

The hourly chart shows selling through $79,000 to near $76,100. The next test is $75,000, while reclaiming $80,000 could ease the pressure—but Thursday's candle has not closed, so the pattern remains unconfirmed.

#5044. Tesla skipped the most important shareholder question on its earnings call

Tesla skipped one of its top shareholder questions on its Q3 2026 earnings call: when Hardware 3 owners will find out if they get a free computer upgrade for the unsupervised “Full Self-Driving” they paid for.

The question ranked fifth by shares on Say. Tesla read and answered questions ranked below it. It went past this one without a word.

Tesla takes retail shareholder questions through the Say platform before every earnings call, and shareholders upvote them with their shares. When we previewed the Q3 2026 earnings as submissions closed, the HW3 question sat fifth with 557 votes and 652,900 shares.

“Tesla has acknowledged that HW3 vehicles cannot support unsupervised FSD. When will owners know whether they can upgrade their hardware for free, and when will Tesla provide a clear plan?”

Head of investor relations Travis Axelrod read nine Say questions on Wednesday’s call. Tesla answered the Cybercab scaling question, unsupervised FSD in customer cars, Cybercab ownership, and robotaxi and Optimus unit economics. Then it went on to questions about Optimus training data, Optimus revenue, FSD mapping, and factory production rates.

The Optimus revenue and FSD mapping questions both ranked below HW3 in every ranking published before the call. The Optimus training data one didn’t even make the top 10.

Axelrod closed the segment with “That’s it for the Say.com questions.” No analyst asked about HW3 either, and HW3, AI3, or a retrofit didn’t come up once during the entire call.

The second question Tesla took was “When can we anticipate FSD unsupervised in customer cars?”

Ashok Elluswamy, Tesla’s head of AI, answered that the same software running in Model Y robotaxis and the Cybercab will come to customers with FSD v15, and that “somewhere around Q2 next year” the first customer vehicles could join the Robotaxi fleet.

That’s another delay, a new timeline that history suggests will likely be pushed back, and an HW4 answer. Musk admitted on the Q1 2026 call that “Hardware 3 simply does not have the capability to achieve unsupervised FSD,” citing an eighth of HW4’s memory bandwidth. So every time Tesla gives a date for unsupervised FSD, it’s a date that doesn’t apply to millions of cars Tesla sold as having all the hardware needed for full self-driving.

And HW3 owners have been promised something by Musk on each of the last two calls. In April, he talked about “microfactories” in major cities to swap computers and cameras, and a discounted trade-in. In July, when a Truist analyst asked about it, Musk said upgrading would make sense “at some point” and floated skipping HW4 for a next-gen board or AI5, which goes to Optimus first.

No cost, no timeline, no process. And this quarter, not even a mention.

Tesla sold FSD for up to $15,000 on the promise that its cars already had the hardware. Now it’s facing class actions in the US, China, and Australia, and a Dutch collective claim that grew to nearly 7,000 HW3 owners with a law firm behind it. Documents from Dutch regulator RDW show Tesla only submitted HW4 for FSD approval in Europe.

HW3 owners got a stripped-down FSD v14 “Lite” this year. It’s a supervised driver assist, and it doesn’t come close to what they bought.

We don’t think this was an accident. Tesla picks which Say questions it reads and in what order, and it chose to answer a question about Optimus revenue it said it had “kind of already answered” over one backed by 650,000 shares about the product its customers already paid for.

Of course they will avoid the question so they can delay this as long as possible and hope the existing owners will just give up or the cars will just work themselves out of the market with write offs over time. That is a lot of money Tesla would have to spend to upgrade the HW that they never intend to.

Any answer Tesla gives on HW3 has a dollar figure attached, and it’s probably a big one: replacing the computer and cameras in what we estimate to be around 4 million cars, or compensating owners. Every statement on it can also end up quoted in court. Saying nothing is cheaper, at least this quarter.

But Tesla can’t have it both ways. It can’t tell shareholders that unsupervised FSD is coming to customer cars by Q2 2027 while ignoring the owners who bought that exact feature years ago and whose cars will never get it. Many of them paid up to $15,000 upfront for it.

The HW3 upgrade isn’t an engineering problem at this point. Musk already said the hardware can’t do it. It’s a question of whether Tesla will pay to honor what it sold, and right now the answer looks like silence. Will an HW3 owner ever get a real plan before a judge forces one? I bet on nope.

#5041. Bitcoin, Ether ETFs draw $128M as crypto rebounds after midterms

Fidelity led Bitcoin ETF inflows, while BlackRock’s IBIT shed another $201 million, extending its outflow streak to four trading sessions.

US-listed spot Bitcoin and Ether exchange-traded funds (ETFs) snapped their outflow streaks on Wednesday as cryptocurrency markets rebounded following the US midterm elections.

Bitcoin (BTC) ETFs attracted $74.6 million on Wednesday, while Ether (ETH) funds added $53 million, according to SoSoValue data.

The turnaround ended three consecutive trading sessions of Bitcoin ETF outflows totaling $1.16 billion, while Ether ETFs snapped a seven-day outflow streak of $657 million.

Bitcoin rebounded above $80,000 and US stocks rallied following Democrats’ midterm election victories on Wednesday.

The latest batch of inflows followed a slowdown in withdrawals, from $399 million on Monday to $116 million on Tuesday.

Blackrock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded another $201.2 million in outflows on Wednesday, extending its four-session outflow streak to $935.2 million, according to Farside Investors data. Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC), meanwhile, led inflows with roughly $237 million.

Daily spot Bitcoin ETF flows by issuer since Oct. 30. Source: Farside Investors

Wednesday’s inflows narrowed Bitcoin’s 2026 net outflows to around $79 million.

James Butterfill, head of research at digital asset manager CoinShares, said Bitcoin had largely priced in the possibility of a Democratic sweep before the election, leaving room for a relief rally as uncertainty eased. He cautioned against attributing too much of Bitcoin’s performance to the election outcome.

CK Zheng, co-founder of crypto hedge fund ZX Squared Capital and former global head of risk at Credit Suisse, pointed to monetary liquidity, institutional investment flows and broader investor appetite for risk as more immediate drivers of Bitcoin’s price.

Zheng said spot Bitcoin ETFs and corporate treasury holdings could broaden demand while also exposing BTC to institutional selling pressure.

#5040. sonnen files for insolvency in Germany as US sales double

Residential battery storage maker sonnen is reporting two very different stories today: Its German business has filed for insolvency, while sonnen USA says sales have doubled and it’s preparing to expand.

The insolvency filing comes just two months after Shell sold sonnen to investment group TIVEN. Germany’s Handelsblatt reported that sonnen GmbH confirmed the filing and informed roughly 400 employees in Wildpoldsried, Bavaria, on October 22.

For homeowners with sonnen batteries, the immediate question is what happens to service. According to Handelsblatt, the company intends to keep supporting its more than 150,000 existing customers. But a substantial overhaul is coming: Numerous subsidiaries are expected to be wound down or enter insolvency proceedings under self-administration, with only one production site remaining. The report does not identify that site.

Meanwhile, sonnen USA’s announcement describes a growing home battery business. The company says sales have doubled and production has tripled year over year, supported by expanded manufacturing in Stone Mountain, Georgia. It expects to deliver more than 180 megawatt-hours of residential battery storage in 2026.

Much of that growth comes from virtual power plants (VPPs). These connect home batteries so they can work together to support the grid, giving households another way to earn value from their solar and storage systems.

In Texas, sonnen says its SOLRITE offering serves more than 6,000 homes, combining rooftop solar, batteries, energy management, and VPP participation. In Massachusetts, its sonnenCommunity program offers cash rewards to participating homeowners in National Grid and Eversource territories. The company also reports rising sales through its Wattsmart program in Utah.

TIVEN bought the entire global business, including sonnen USA. US CEO Blake Richetta stated today that “TIVEN is firmly committed to the long-term success of sonnen USA as a strong, market-leading company in the North American VPP energy market.”

TIVEN stated that it “looks forward to bolstering the company’s impressive growth.”

But the US announcement leaves an important question unanswered: How will the German restructuring affect the US operation and its customers? It doesn’t address the insolvency filing, and sonnen USA declined to comment on developments in Germany when contacted by pv magazine.

The US growth figures show demand for sonnen’s batteries. What customers still need is a clear explanation of how manufacturing, software support, and service will be maintained through the restructuring.

#5039. France budget rejection sidelines stablecoin tax, wallet reporting

Three adopted amendments, including stablecoin taxation and self-custody reporting, fall away but could be reintroduced in the Senate.

The French National Assembly rejected the 2027 state budget’s revenue section late Wednesday, removing crypto-related amendments from the version of the bill sent to the Senate.

The Assembly rejected the revenue section by a vote of 458–0 at first reading, with two abstentions, according to the official voting record. The revenue section covers government income, such as taxes.

After the rejection, the government will send the Senate the original budget text, without the crypto-related amendments adopted by lawmakers.

The proposals could still be reintroduced in the Senate and would need to survive the remaining budget process to become law.

Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph before the vote that rejection of the revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments.”

The Assembly adopted three crypto-related amendments before rejecting the revenue section.

On Oct. 16, lawmakers adopted an amendment that would make gains from crypto-to-stablecoin conversions taxable.

On Tuesday, lawmakers adopted another amendment that would require taxpayers to disclose self-custodied crypto holdings totaling at least 100,000 euros ($112,000), with the threshold assessed on Dec. 31 of the tax year.

Another amendment would increase penalties from 10,000 euros to 50,000 euros for crypto platforms failing to provide information requested by tax authorities.

The Senate is scheduled to begin debating the finance bill on Nov. 20, followed by an examination of the revenue section’s articles on Nov. 23, according to its schedule.
