#5036. Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

House Democrats are reportedly set to progress crypto legislation after sweeping the U.S. midterms.

There are plans to expand the Crypto Investor Consumer Bill of Rights—a Clarity Act amendment led by Dem. Maxine Waters—into a broader market structure bill, journalist Eleanor Terrett tweeted. The proposal would include giving the SEC more power to supervise the crypto markets and limiting the role of the CFTC, sources told Terrett.

🚨SCOOP: House Democrats are discussing plans to expand the Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, @RepMaxineWaters’ amendment to the Clarity Act in 2025, into a broader crypto “market structure” bill next Congress.

The proposal would fold in Democratic Clarity Act…

— Eleanor Terrett (@EleanorTerrett) November 4, 2026

On the surface, the midterm election results could be seen as a major hit to crypto policy in the U.S., with President Trump's Republican administration widely regarded as the pro-crypto party, while the Democrats were deemed more hostile. However, experts, told Decrypt, the tide might be turning and crypto policy evolving into a more bipartisan issue.

Advocacy organization Stand With Crypto endorsed 45 candidates in the midterms, per its election tracker, with 27 pro-crypto candidate wins, 12 losses, and six races still live at the time of writing. Nearly half of the endorsed candidates were Democrats.

"We’re proud to see a majority of our endorsed candidates win their elections," Mason Lynaugh, executive director of Stand With Crypto, said in a statement shared with Decrypt. "To the candidates who won’t return to Washington, thank you for going to bat for crypto-owning Americans when it counted."

Congratulations to the pro-crypto champions who won their elections, including more than 50 pro-crypto new members heading to Congress.

In the closest races, our advocates showed up: in Florida's 9th District, Darren Soto won by 1,477 votes. In that same district, we mobilized… pic.twitter.com/lcq055GNET

— Stand With Crypto🛡️ (@standwithcrypto) November 4, 2026

James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares, described Republican Bryan Steil as a "meaningful loss" for crypto. The Wisconsin Congressman was the chair of the House digital assets subcommittee and closely involved in efforts to advance the Clarity Act—which were stymied when it stalled in the Senate earlier this year.

That said, Butterfill also highlighted several winners—Democrats Chris Pappas in New Hampshire and James Talarico in Texas, as well as Wyoming Republican Harriet Hageman—who have supported crypto policy. He stressed, however, that the challenge is now "converting" that support into passing legislation. Some believe that could slow an already difficult path for the Clarity Act.

"I wouldn’t say it’s game over for Clarity, but passing it has become tougher," Carlos Guzman, vice president of research at crypto trading firm GSR, told Decrypt. "Some Democrats support clearer crypto rules, but I wouldn’t expect it to be high on their agenda. Any agreement will likely require stronger provisions on things like consumer protection and conflicts of interest."

The Clarity Act looks to establish rules for crypto markets and has been batted back and forth since 2025. Democrats have pushed back on the bill, citing Trump's crypto conflicts of interest, with Senator Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) pushing for the bill to ban officials and their families from profiting from the sector.

"For other crypto policies, the election doesn’t automatically replace the leadership of the SEC or CFTC or stop their rulemaking," Guzman said. "Congressional oversight does change, however, so we might see more scrutiny of regulatory decisions, with Democrats also able to influence agency funding and future appointments."

Butterfill added that he believes crypto is becoming less dependent on the Clarity Act, as the SEC and CFTC push for regulatory clarity without the need for Congress. He also said that AI regulation may take eyes away from crypto, slowing crypto policy in the process.

Following the midterms, Bitcoin has exhibited volatility, and is currently trading at around $76,820, down 4.1% on the day, per Decrypt data.

"The result alone would not make me change my medium-term Bitcoin outlook," Butterfill told Decrypt. "Slower reform could delay some institutional activity, but we would expect the more direct impact to fall on US crypto businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. We would revisit the outlook if political uncertainty materially weakened investment flows or tightened financial conditions."

#5035. South Korea seeks 4th crypto tax delay, targets 2029 start

South Korea is seeking a fourth delay to its 22% crypto tax, months after reaffirming plans to begin taxing digital asset gains in January 2027.

South Korea’s government has backed postponing tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029, in what would be the fourth delay to its implementation.

Finance Minister Lee Hyoung-il announced the proposal Thursday, saying the government would consult lawmakers on postponing the January 2027 implementation date.

The tax would impose a 22% levy, comprising a 20% income tax and 2% local tax, on annual crypto gains exceeding 2.5 million won ($1,860).

The government cited 2029 as the start of information exchange with the US under the Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework (CARF). It said imposing the tax before those exchanges begin could put the domestic market at a disadvantage relative to overseas markets. It also wants to account for changes under planned digital asset legislation.

Officials also said they plan to introduce a three-year loss carryforward provision, allowing investors to offset crypto losses against profits earned over the following three years. The government plans to submit amendments to improve the tax system during parliament’s regular session in 2027, with detailed tax guidelines planned for the first half of 2028.

The tax was originally scheduled to take effect in January 2022 but was subsequently postponed to 2023, 2025 and 2027.

The proposal reverses the government’s previous position. A Finance Ministry official said in May that implementation would proceed in 2027.

South Korean finance officials recently engaged with major crypto exchanges amid growing calls to postpone the tax. Industry representatives cited unresolved tax rules for different transaction types and insufficient time to prepare the necessary infrastructure.

Related: South Korea plans cybersecurity overhaul after attacks hit banks, crypto firms

#5034. Another megawatt-level charger enters China: 300 miles in 5 minutes

Chery has announced a 1.44 megawatt charging system, able to deliver 500km (311 miles) in 5 minutes. And it’s not even the fastest system available in China.

It’s become common knowledge at this point that Chinese EVs are the world’s state of the art, but the country isn’t sitting on its laurels, it’s still racing to compete with newer and better technology.

A case in point has been the country’s DC fast chargers, where we’ve seen a slew of announcements of new systems in just the last year.

BYD has a 1.5MW charger which it has already installed 10,000 of, Geely just announced a 2.2MW charger, and CATL has a hyper-fast charging battery.

The competition is so fierce that firms are starting to compete over seconds, not minutes of charging time.

And now, a new challenger appears: Chery.

Chery announced a 1.44MW charger that can add 500km (311miles) of range to a battery in 5 minutes, and can charge from 10-97% in 6 minutes and 50 seconds, as reported by CarNewsChina.

The numbers put it in league with the best of the best in the country. Per CarNewsChina’s accounting, the new Chery “Volt-Dragon” battery is the second-fastest at reaching 97% state of charge, 23 seconds behind CATL’s 6 minute, 27 second record time. FAW, Geely and BYD are all between 8 and 9 minutes for the same full charge metric.

Some other firms released times for charging in very cold or very warm weather, which Chery declined to release during its announcement. Though it did say that it could still deliver 500km of range in 8 minutes in “conservative scenarios.”

Could it be the drugged out Elon Musk is more into politics and taking over the world than advancing EV technology at an electric car company.

In Chery’s announcement, it dropped the same buzzword as Geely did: “AI-Powered.” Chery calls its system CheryMind, and supposedly it adjusts charging power based on temperature changes within the battery, to help maintain optimal charging speeds and battery health.

Meanwhile in the US, the fastest public chargers are around 400kW, with 750kW potentially coming next year. That’s all plenty quick enough for refueling, especially on a roadtrip, but for high-throughput DC charging locations, especially those in urban areas where some people might not have a consistent parking spot to charge in (and yes, we need to fix that), higher speed chargers can relieve a lot of pressure.

Chery has only just started installing chargers, and says it will have 100 chargers open in 10 Chinese cities soon. It plans to install 20,000 stations worldwide by 2029, which is quite a bit slower than BYD’s target to have 20,000 chargers installed by the end of this year.

#5030. Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs is betting the network’s next wave of traders wants more than meme coins.

Leap, a new company under Solana Labs, launched a beta of its mobile trading app Thursday, letting users trade tokenized stocks, ETFs, metals, currencies, and crypto in one place, around the clock and subject to restrictions.

The app is available in early access on iPhone through Apple’s public TestFlight program, with an Android version coming soon. It isn’t available in the U.K. or other restricted regions.

Leap is built by the team behind Dialect, which Solana Labs acquired earlier this year. Dialect developed Solana Actions and Blinks, a protocol that turns blockchain transactions into shareable links, which launched in 2024, as well as the cashtags feature on X.

The company is pitching the app as an alternative to fast-paced meme coin trading, aimed at people who want to make better-informed decisions over the long term. Its research team curates market-moving news and pins each story to the price chart at the moment it broke.

The app tracks profit and loss across every deposit, withdrawal, trade and fee, including each position’s breakeven price. Users enter an amount in dollars, and each trade executes as an onchain swap.

Leap uses Jupiter for trade routing, Helius for infrastructure and Birdeye for market data.

“The Solana blockchain now confirms a trade in about 150 milliseconds thanks to Alpenglow, so the network is now faster than ever,” said Chris Osborn, Leap’s general manager and Dialect’s founder. Alpenglow a consensus overhaul designed to sharply cut Solana’s confirmation times.

“Every app that brings new people onchain makes the whole ecosystem stronger,” said Solana Labs co-founder and CEO Anatoly Yakovenko.

Leap isn’t charging its own fees at launch, though network and routing costs apply. It plans to use future fees to buy and burn SOL, permanently removing tokens from circulation, and to fund a referral program. Early waitlist members, dubbed the Founders Club, would earn 30% of the trading fees generated by users they invite and 5% from those users’ own referrals.

Leap arrives as tokenized stocks gain traction on Solana and beyond. Securitize has brought tokenized Nvidia, Apple, and Amazon shares to Solana. The trend has also met pushback: 56 companies have objected to having their shares traded on OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

#5029. Bitcoin win rate vs. S&P 500 recovers as post-midterm history favors stocks

Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in half of the past 90 US trading sessions, while historical midterm data highlights gains for stocks.

Bitcoin (BTC) has outperformed the S&P 500 in half of US trading sessions over Glassnode’s latest measurement period, ending a prolonged stretch of losing more sessions than it won.

Data from onchain analytics platform Glassnode shows that Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in 50% of Wall Street trading sessions over a rolling 90-session period.

The reading, covering the 90 sessions through Wednesday, marks an improvement after an extended period in which Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won.

“The win rate has not been above 50% since March 2025; for most of the past year, Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won,” Glassnode wrote in the latest edition of its regular newsletter, The Week Onchain.

BTC/USD vs. S&P 500 per-session returns. Source: Glassnode

Glassnode added that Bitcoin’s average per-session gains were also “slightly higher” than those of the S&P 500.

Democrats’ wins in the US midterm elections have also drawn attention to historical stock market gains following similar results.

Data from crypto asset manager Capriole Investments shows that Democratic sweeps of Congress under Republican presidents have historically been followed by strong S&P 500 returns.

Related: Bitcoin hits $80.5K as US stocks rally on midterm election results

Of the 26 midterm elections since 1922 (excluding this year’s vote), Democrats won both the House and Senate under a Republican president seven times. The S&P 500 gained an average of 10.7% over the following three months.

Average gains were 17.5% after six months and 15.5% after a year.

“There is simply no better election result for markets than we just had,” Capriole founder Charles Edwards commented on the data in a post on X.

S&P 500 performance based on Midterm Election outcome. Source: Charles Edwards on X.com

Data from TradingView puts Bitcoin’s rolling 20-day correlation with the S&P 500 at 0.63. The measure has become markedly positive since August, when both crypto and stocks gained as the US Treasury expanded its debt buybacks.

BTC/USD one-day chart with S&P 500 correlation data. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#5027. Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood studios and production companies have been quietly issuing guidelines barring employees from betting on prediction markets, entertainment industry trade paper The Hollywood Reporter reported on Thursday.

The warnings prohibit staff from betting on events such as studio earnings, casting decisions and release dates, or passing information to friends and family so they can bet instead, according to the report. At least one company has reportedly set up a hotline for employees to anonymously report colleagues suspected of placing bets.

The Hollywood Reporter's own parent company, Penske Media, has also updated its employee handbook in recent weeks to ban bets on "award nominations, casting decisions, production timelines and release dates," the outlet said.

The rules extend long-standing bans on insider trading in the stock market to platforms such as Kalshi and Polymarket, which let users bet on everything from who will be cast as the next James Bond to who will be voted off a reality show.

"You now have, essentially, an explosion in the number of employers who have to think about insider trading rules from an ethical standpoint," Joshua Mitts, a law professor at Columbia University who studies prediction markets, told the outlet.

Enforcing those rules may be another matter. "With blockchain-based trading, it can be basically impossible if you know how to use a mixer correctly and know how to hide your identity behind multiple layers of intermediation," Mitts said.

Some bookmakers are already spooked. Betting site BetOnline temporarily pulled its market on the next James Bond this week after a sudden wave of "sharp money" pushed actor Callum Turner above longtime favorite Jack Lowden, raising concerns about a leak, according to the report.

Entertainment insiders have been caught before. In February, Kalshi said a video editor at MrBeast's company had made about $5,000 using confidential information to bet on MrBeast content; he was fired and fined $20,000.

Federal prosecutors have also brought cases over insider trading on prediction markets. In April, an active-duty U.S. soldier was charged with using classified information about the raid to capture Nicolás Maduro to bet on Polymarket. In May, a Google engineer was charged with commodities fraud, wire fraud and money laundering over Polymarket bets allegedly placed using confidential company data. Both cases are pending.

Governments have moved too. California, New York and Illinois have banned state employees from using nonpublic information to bet on prediction markets, while the platforms have rolled out their own surveillance tools. Polymarket has investigated dozens of bets on the U.S. midterm elections.

One kind of entertainment bet is already off the table. Under the Onion Futures Act of 1958, U.S. law explicitly bans just two types of futures contracts: those on onions and those on movie box office receipts, The Hollywood Reporter noted.

#5025. Overhead wired charging comes to Austin electric bus fleet

CapMetro’s Austin transit fleets electrified two public bus lines and opened two new park-and-ride facilities featuring overhead pantograph-style wired charging equipment to help Austinites breathe a little bit easier.

Originally announced in June, CapMetro’s two new park-and-ride stations are now open. Dubbed the Goodnight Ranch and Expo Center facilities debuted alongside the agency’s Rapid 800 and 837 electric bus routes, adding upgraded passenger amenities and end-of-line charging infrastructure to support its growing fleet of electric transit buses.

At CapMetro’s main bus depot, infrastructure partner Camber Charging supplied and installed two 1,440 kW charging systems connected to 48 industrial dispensers. The installation provides 2.9 MW of total charging capacity, and is designed to support overnight charging for up to 48 electric buses simultaneously.

The new Goodnight Ranch and Expo Center terminals extend that infrastructure along the Rapid 800 and 837 routes with the overhead pantograph chargers that are the big news here, allowing buses to top off their batteries at the end of each line and eliminate any lingering range anxiety for the trip back.

“Instead of a typical bus holding 8 hours of charging, on-route charging takes that out to 20 to 22 hours,” explains CapMetro Chief Operations Officer and Executive VP, Andy Skabowski. “Every time that pantograph drops, we charge that bus. That’s what makes a commitment like (every) 10- to 15-minute service possible.”

The buses themselves are built on Canadian manufacturer New Flyer’s Xcelsior battery-electric transit platform (battery specs aren’t included in the release, but New Flyer claims up to 660 kWh of available capacity). And, with up to 320 all-electric hp with all its torque available at 0 rpm, the city’s 48 electric buses will have no problem navigating Austin’s stop-and-go traffic.

This seems like a logical cost effective solution. In theory, this should enable transit systems to purchase busses with smaller batteries which would surely offset the cost of the new charging infrastructure.

Camber Charging provides preventive maintenance, charger and energy management software and remote diagnostics across all three locations.

SOURCE | IMAGES: CapMetro, via Charged EVs.

#5024. What You Should Know About Exercising When Bone Density Is a Concern

Osteopenia or osteoporosis doesn’t have to sideline your workouts. Here’s what to do, how to modify, and when to back off.

You might have heard that being active is a good idea when you have osteopenia or osteoporosis, conditions marked by lower-than-normal bone density. But when you’re exercising specifically to improve bone health, some types of exercise may be more helpful than others. Before lacing up your sneakers, there are a few things to know.

“When patients ask me what they can do to preserve and protect their bone health, consistent weight bearing and resistance exercises are the first things I recommend,” says Nicole Leung, MD, director of the Osteoporosis and Bone Health Program at NYU Langone Health in New York City. “Bone is a living tissue, so it adapts to mechanical load through remodeling.”

When bone tissue is stressed via physical activity, your body responds by making more bone, helping strengthen the tissue. Exercise also lowers the activity of cells that dissolve and break down old bone. That combo helps preserve or even improve your bone mineral density, regardless of your health status. Exercise may slow or even prevent the progression of osteoporosis; if you have osteoporosis already, it can help you maintain the bone you have and reduce your risk for falls and fractures.

A bone-density diagnosis doesn’t mean you have to give up exercise—but your routine should reflect your fracture risk and health history. Here’s what Dr. Leung recommends for choosing activities, adapting your routine, and knowing when to check with your doctor before making major changes.

There are a few types of activities that make up a bone-friendly exercise plan, and it’s important to get the right amount of each. Here are the types of exercise to do regularly.

These are exercises where you’re on your feet and putting force on your muscles and bones as they work against gravity. Depending on factors like your age, fitness level, and bone mineral density, these activities can include:

People working to maintain their bone health should do these sorts of activities for 30 to 60 minutes, three to five days per week, according to the American College of Sports Medicine.

As for activities like swimming and cycling, they’re great for getting your heart pumping—but because they’re low-impact, they won’t do much for your bones. That said, feel free to do them in addition to your weight-bearing exercises if you enjoy them.

Lifting (relatively) heavy things works your muscles and helps build bone density. This includes free weights, machines, resistance bands, or body weight exercises like squats or push-ups. Aim for two to three strength sessions per week, and don’t be afraid to gradually go heavier (with your doctor’s okay). Pushing yourself to 70 to 90 percent of your max effort may deliver a bigger bone boost than lifting lighter weights, research suggests.

Having osteoporosis makes you more prone to fractures after falls, but you can strengthen your balance—and reduce your chances for a broken bone—with exercises like yoga, tai chi, or Pilates. “Any exercise where you have to shift your body weight side to side and forward and back can be very helpful for your stabilizing muscles,” Dr. Leung says. She recommends working balance exercises into your schedule a few times per week. Find safe modifications if you’re worried about falling, like holding onto a sturdy chair or touching a wall.

Whether you’ve been exercising for years, are just starting out, or are returning to a routine after a long hiatus, Dr. Leung advises paying attention to your posture, avoiding exercise when you’re overly exhausted, and increasing intensity gradually. Older women who are new to exercise can still do resistance training, she says, but may need to start at a slower pace and under supervision.

Despite the overall benefits of exercise, you still need to be careful, particularly when you have osteoporosis. Certain activities can set the stage for potential spine fractures, so before starting an exercise program, decide with your doctor or other qualified professional what is and isn’t safe for you.

According to the International Osteoporosis Foundation, you may need to limit or avoid moves that involve:

Yoga, while helpful for balance, can involve poses that put your spine at risk (like forward bends, deep twists, plow pose, shoulder stand, and pigeon pose). If you decide to give it a try, choose a class where the teacher can offer safe modifications, Dr. Leung says. It’s also best to steer clear of activities that come with a high risk of falling from above standing height, like skiing or horseback riding, she adds.

When it comes to stressing your bones, it’s possible to get too much of a good thing. “Overburdening your bone can actually lead to stress fractures,” Dr. Leung says. If you do prolonged intense weight-bearing and high-impact exercises like jogging or jumping rope, avoid scheduling them on back-to-back days. Alternate those workouts with activities like strength training, or have an active recovery day where you walk or do yoga or tai chi, she recommends.

Make it a point to stay active even after you’ve completed the day’s workout. According to Dr. Leung, non-exercise activities like casual walking or doing chores may help to maintain bone density when compared to sedentary behavior. Stroll over to chat with a coworker at their desk during a break, take the stairs when you can, or walk while you call a friend instead of sitting. A few minutes here and there can really add up.

Any new or worsening bone or spine pain is a warning sign to stop exercising and contact your doctor. You should also call your doctor if you fall while exercising, notice that you’ve had height loss, or if your spine appears curved, which could be signs of a spinal fracture. And always stop exercising and seek medical attention if you experience other severe symptoms like dizziness, faintness, or chest pain.

Consistency counts. People with low bone density who stay active over several months tend to see the greatest bone-health benefits, studies show. Choose activities you enjoy, build up gradually, and find a rhythm you can sustain. The goal isn’t to nail every workout—it’s to keep movement in your life.

#5021. Yozma IN 11 review: The OG mini e-moto gets updates galore

…including 4.5kW motor, bigger 1.4kWh battery and 45mph top speed

Yozma may not have invented the mini moto space, but they certainly helped mainstream it. Together with Tuttios and Sur Rons, Yozma’s IN10 was one of the most popular mini motos out there, and if you surveyed the average group of 14-15-year-olds in a suburban rideout, you’d be hard-pressed not to find at least a few Yozma IN10s among them…

Today Yozma is shipping the IN 11 ($100 off with code 9TO5TOYS100), which attempts to bridge the gap between entry-level mini-bikes and high-end off-roaders, but at what cost?

Taken in a vacuum, the Yozma IN11 is an impressive update to the IN 10 and actually is more closely specced to the bigger IN10 Pro.

But we of course, don’t live in a vacuum, and others, including the Heybike Villain and the Engwe Eagle have already brought 52V, 4.5kW motors to the pit bike form factor, sometimes at much lower cost.

I should say here that parents who want to keep their kids’ e-motos in the 20-30mph range are going to be a whole lot happier with the 2.6kW than 4.5kW motors. Two nice safety improvements over the original Yozma IN10 include the magnetic kill switch and brake lights on the IN11.

If we are keeping it real here, there are dozens of similar-sized and spec’d mini-e-motos out there, often at a much lower cost than the Yozma IN11. But I’d argue that as the biggest and most trusted of these brands, Yozma is a safer bet.

Also, the IN11 is just a lot more solid than the random Amazon/Alibaba electric pit bike. Putting the IN11 together is quicker and easier than any other emoto I’ve built, and it just feels a lot more solid.

That feeling extends to the safety features. The brakes are more dialed in. The turning feels a lot tighter, and the overall ride is quieter and lighter. I believe, and my limited testing bears this out, that spec for spec, I got a lot more range on the IN11 than I got on other bikes with similar battery and motor specs. That 200nm of torque feels like it belongs on a much more expensive e-moto.

The smart wheelie mode is a nice trick (15, 25, or 40 degrees wheelie settings) and something we’ve only seen on Anylands for this price point so far. It is less of a standout feature on the IN11, and we expect to see this on just about every new pit bike coming out over the next few years. In fact, we’ve got another one in the review queue now.

That said, the price, which is currently fluctuating between $1299 and $1499 (don’t forget your $100 off 9TO5TOYS100 code), Yozma is going to be on the more expensive side of the 4.5kW emotos out there and some of the less established and more sketchy newcomers are bringing an intense value competition to the place where Yozma once dominated by itself.

#5020. S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

S&P 500 Plunges, Wall Street Fears Circuit Breaker, Just One Day After Trump Celebrated Record Highs

President Donald Trump's celebration of Wall Street's highs aged rather poorly. Less than 24 hours after boasting about Americans' retirement accounts going "through the roof," the S&P 500 plunged as much as 3.8% Thursday, bringing emergency trading halts into the conversation.

The selloff prompted traders to monitor the market's circuit-breaker thresholds, which can temporarily shut down trading across U.S. stock exchanges when losses become too severe. The S&P 500 would need to fall another 3.2 percentage points from its decline to trigger the first emergency halt.

The timing is particularly awkward for Trump, who has repeatedly pointed to stock market performance as evidence of his administration's economic success.

"Stock Market just hit another Record High—91 Days in just a short period of time—Less than two years. Your 401(k)s are through the roof," Trump wrote Wednesday on his Truth Social account. He closed the message with "Record Setting All! President DONALD J. TRUMP."

Wall Street had rallied Wednesday following the Democratic Party's sweeping midterm election victories. The S&P 500 gained 1.6%, its strongest daily performance in three months, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average jumped more than 1,500 points to 53.361 points.

Thursday's losses erase that rally in a single session, with technology stocks taking the biggest beating.

The U.S. stock market has three emergency thresholds designed to slow panic selling. These mechanisms, known as circuit breakers, automatically suspend trading when the S&P 500 falls by a specified percentage from its previous closing price.

The first threshold is a 7% decline, corresponding to an S&P 500 level of 7,150.41 points for Thursday's session. Reaching that level before 3:25 p.m. ET would trigger a 15-minute halt across US equity markets.

The circuit breaker level is located in the following chart’s blue line

A 13% decline, or 6,689.09 points, would trigger another 15-minute suspension. The most severe threshold comes at 20%, or 6,150.89 points, which would shut down trading for the remainder of the day.

Under the New York Stock Exchange's rules, the first two circuit breakers stop applying at 3:25 p.m. ET, while the 20% threshold remains active throughout the session.

The rules apply to the entire U.S. equity market, not just stocks included in the S&P 500. A market-wide halt would also suspend trading in companies listed on the Nasdaq and other US exchanges.

The technology sector was particularly hard hit as investors continued digesting the consequences of Tuesday's election results. At around 11 a.m. ET, the Nasdaq Composite had fallen nearly 5%, while the S&P 500 was down 3.2%.

Nvidia lost more than 6%, while the broader semiconductor index plunged nearly 11%. Companies involved in building AI infrastructure were also under pressure as investors worried that Democratic victories could bring stricter oversight of data-center construction.

"We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout," Ipek Ozkardeskaya, senior analyst at Swissquote Bank, told Reuters.

The selloff also spilled into cryptocurrencies. Bitcoin fell below $76,000, wiping out its post-election recovery as approximately $428 million in leveraged crypto long positions were liquidated within an hour, as Decrypt reported earlier today.

Stocks also faced pressure from persistent inflation concerns and uncertainty over the Federal Reserve's next interest-rate decision. Investors are awaiting Friday's October employment report, which could provide further clues about the direction of monetary policy.

Despite Thursday's sharp losses, the S&P 500 had not reached its first market-wide circuit breaker at the time of this report.

#5019. Thailand to block Bitget in December over unlicensed crypto trading

Thailand’s SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget’s operator, with access to its website and app set to be blocked from Dec. 5.

Thailand’s securities regulator has filed a criminal complaint against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, for allegedly operating without a license.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said Thursday that it had notified the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society to block access to Bitget from Dec. 5. The restriction will cover Bitget’s website, other associated domains and its mobile app.

The regulator advised users to take action regarding assets held on the platform before the deadline.

The complaint follows Bitget’s Oct. 20 announcement of plans to acquire a licensed Thai crypto exchange.

According to the SEC, Bitget offered crypto trading services between 2022 and 2025, charged trading fees and promoted its services in Thai through its websites and social media channels. The regulator said these activities violated Thailand’s digital asset business licensing requirements.

The complaint also names Kritsana Kritsananuwat, whom the SEC accused of helping promote Bitget through Facebook and YouTube. The watchdog previously filed a criminal complaint against him in March 2025 over allegations that he helped promote OKX’s unlicensed exchange activities.

Cointelegraph attempted to contact Kritsananuwat through a phone number listed for advertising inquiries in a video on his YouTube channel but was unable to reach him.

Thailand introduced licensing requirements for digital asset businesses in 2018 and tightened the rules in April 2025 to cover foreign platforms targeting Thai users. Changes to a separate cybercrime law also made it easier for the ministry to block access to unlicensed platforms.

Related: Thailand SEC says several companies eye crypto ETFs, none has applied

The regulator filed the complaint with Thailand’s Economic Crime Suppression Division for investigation. Prosecutors will decide whether to bring charges, while courts will determine criminal liability.

Bitget did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

Bitget announced plans on Oct. 20 to acquire GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics, a locally licensed crypto exchange and brokerage, through BTGT Management (Thailand), a company in which Bitget said it held an interest.

The proposed acquisition remained subject to regulatory approval and other conditions at the time of the disclosure. Bitget said it intended to introduce its trading platform to Thai users after completing the transaction and obtaining approval for the change of control.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said at the time.

#5015. Trump’s memecoin ventures derailed CLARITY Act, Scaramucci says

The SkyBridge founder said disputes over ethics restrictions also stalled negotiations, with both sides responsible for the setback.

SkyBridge Capital founder Anthony Scaramucci blamed US President Donald Trump’s crypto business interests and memecoin launches for derailing the CLARITY Act, saying they cost the legislation Democratic support.

Speaking with Cointelegraph at SALT London, Scaramucci said the memecoin launches shortly before Trump’s January 2025 inauguration undermined bipartisan momentum for crypto regulation following the 2024 election.

“Singular reason,” Scaramucci told Cointelegraph when asked whether the memecoin activities were the sole reason the bill did not pass. He said Democrats who had become more receptive to crypto regulation withdrew their support following the launches.

The remarks follow the Senate’s failure to advance the CLARITY Act on Sept. 15, when a procedural motion received 49 votes in favor and 50 against, falling short of the 60 votes required. The bill, which sought to establish a federal regulatory framework for digital assets, faced disagreements over ethics provisions restricting government officials’ crypto interests.

Scaramucci also pointed to disagreements over proposed ethics restrictions, saying lawmakers sought stronger provisions that the White House opposed.

“I think it’s the business interests, the memecoins,” Scaramucci said, adding that lawmakers were “trying to put real teeth into the ethics provision” but the White House did not want to sign it.

“There’s culpability on both sides for that deal not going forward,” he said.

Related: Market participants weigh in on SEC’s proposed crypto regulations

In July, five Democratic senators called for hearings into Trump’s crypto holdings and potential conflicts of interest, citing concerns that his financial ties could influence crypto policy. House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill also acknowledged that Trump’s crypto businesses had complicated efforts to pass legislation.

In August, consumer advocacy group Public Citizen estimated that investors incurred at least $4.7 billion in largely unrealized losses across Trump-related crypto ventures, including $3.2 billion associated with the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin. The group said Trump earned $635 million in memecoin licensing fees in 2025, citing his financial disclosure.

Despite the legislative setback, Scaramucci said the SEC’s approach under Chair Paul Atkins could help tokenization reach “escape velocity,” with enough adoption to make it difficult for a future SEC chair to stop or slow the industry.

He said it was too early to know whether adoption would reach that point before the next administration.

#5014. Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

Trump-Backed American Bitcoin Turns a Profit as Bitcoin Rise Lifts Books

American Bitcoin, the Trump-family-backed mining venture, swung to a roughly $126 million profit in the third quarter, a sharp reversal from the loss it posted three months earlier, as a jump in Bitcoin’s book value and disciplined accumulation powered its best showing yet.

The Nasdaq-listed company, which trades under the ticker ABTC and is majority-owned by miner Hut 8, reported net income of about $126 million for the quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with a $57.2 million net loss in the second quarter.

Adjusted EBITDA came in at roughly $221 million, versus a $45 million loss the prior quarter. Much of the swing came from a $197 million gain on its digital assets, as Bitcoin’s value rose during the period.

The company grew its Bitcoin reserve to about 8,427 BTC, up roughly 425 coins, or 5%, from the end of June, and lifted its “satoshis per share,” a measure of how much Bitcoin backs each share, by about 5%. Notably, it did so without buying any Bitcoin or issuing new shares through its at-the-market program during the quarter, relying instead on its own mining output.

“These are significant results, particularly in a challenging digital asset market,” said Eric Trump, the company’s co-founder and chief strategy officer, touting the profit and the growth in Bitcoin per share. CEO Mike Ho credited “a disciplined, Bitcoin-denominated operating business,” saying the firm continued “mining Bitcoin at a structural discount to spot.”

The mining operation itself had a tougher quarter. American Bitcoin mined about 891 BTC, down from 932 in the prior period, and its cost to mine rose to roughly $42,100 per Bitcoin from $36,500, which it attributed to higher seasonal power costs. Gross margin slipped to 41% from 49%. The company ended the quarter with a fleet of about 89,242 miners and 28.1 exahashes per second of capacity.

The results cap a volatile stretch for the venture. American Bitcoin notched a record mining quarter earlier this year as it crossed 8,000 BTC, but its stock has struggled, hitting new lows and prompting a 1-for-15 reverse stock split in July. Shares have fallen well below their debut price since the company went public through a merger with Gryphon Digital Mining.

The company’s profit, while striking, rests heavily on an accounting gain tied to Bitcoin’s price rather than core mining operations, meaning a downturn in Bitcoin could just as easily swing the figure back into the red.

#5013. Tesla’s $10B solar factory could get 35 years of tax breaks in Texas

Tesla’s proposed $10 billion “Project Crystal Sun” solar factory in Texas could get up to 35 years of local tax breaks on its equipment, according to the four agreements Fort Bend County commissioners approved on Thursday.

The vote was 4-0. And Tesla still hasn’t committed to building the plant in Texas.

As we first reported in August, Tesla applied for a 10-year school property tax limitation under the state’s JETI program, through Lamar CISD and the Texas Comptroller. The plan is a vertically integrated solar plant near Richmond, southwest of Houston, that would make everything from silicon ingots to finished modules, with commercial production targeted for Q1 2029.

Lamar CISD approved the school tax piece in September. The county was next.

On October 22, the commissioners court approved items 25A to 25D on its agenda: a Chapter 312 tax abatement, a Chapter 381 incentive agreement, a foreign trade zone agreement with a payment in lieu of taxes (PILOT), and a “true up” PILOT agreement. Interim County Judge Daniel Wong abstained because his engineering firm has worked with Tesla.

Each agreement covers a different slice of Tesla’s tax bill, and together they run for decades.

The Chapter 312 agreement abates 100% of county property taxes on Tesla’s equipment and other tangible personal property at the plant for 10 tax years. The clock starts in tax year 2029 by default, but Tesla can move it to the first year after production starts, as late as 2031 without needing the county’s approval.

Tesla’s JETI application splits the $10.1 billion into $1.52 billion of real property and $8.6 billion of personal property, mostly production equipment.

Land and buildings aren’t part of the deal. Anything that isn’t eligible for the abatement, including the land and buildings, stays “fully taxable at all times,” according to the agreement.

Inventory goes through the foreign trade zone deal instead. Tesla’s site would be part of Port Freeport’s Foreign-Trade Zone No. 149, which exempts eligible inventory from local property tax. In exchange for the county not objecting, Tesla agreed to make PILOT payments, set at “zero dollars or 0%” of the forgone taxes for as long as the abatement is in effect.

When the abatement ends, the Chapter 381 agreement takes over. Tesla starts paying county taxes on its equipment, and the county pays 90% of them back as an annual grant for 20 years. The county keeps 10% for its general fund.

Inventory follows the same pattern. After the abatement, Tesla’s FTZ payment rises to 10% of the taxes that would otherwise apply.

There’s one more lever. If Tesla averages at least 12,000 employees for any two consecutive years during the first decade, the first five years of the rebate period become a 100% grant, and the rebate period stretches from 20 to 25 years.

That pushes the agreement out to Project Year 35. It’s also 2,288 jobs above the 9,712 Tesla says it will create.

To keep the tax breaks, Tesla has to start construction by June 30, 2027, begin “regular commercial production of solar cells” by December 31, 2030, and invest $10 billion by the later of December 31, 2031 or two years after production starts. Force majeure and “ERCOT Delay” provisions can extend those dates.

The jobs ramp is 2,500, 5,000, 7,500, then 9,712 employees by the fourth year of operations, with an average compensation of $55 per hour. Tesla gets the full abatement as long as it hits 90% of the headcount target, which is about 8,741 people.

The penalties are mild. Missing a jobs or wage target for one year only costs Tesla that year’s break, and two misses in a row lets the county terminate the agreement. Full recapture of abated taxes, plus a possible 15% penalty if they aren’t repaid within 60 days, is reserved for “Material Defaults” like missing the $10 billion investment, blowing the construction deadlines or abandoning the plant for 12 months.

Under the Chapter 381 deal, grants from earlier years can’t be clawed back for a headcount shortfall.

The county’s floor is small. The true-up agreement only guarantees that Fort Bend collects at least $2 million a year, and 25% of the taxes Tesla pays on its real property, the county’s 10% share and the FTZ payments all count toward that total.

For scale, Tesla’s own consultant, Kroll, estimated in the JETI filing that the project would pay $276.5 million in Fort Bend County general taxes over 38 years without incentives, peaking at $16.2 million in 2030. That estimate lumps land, buildings and equipment together. The equipment portion is the one the county just agreed to give back, for most of those years.

In its application, it says the economics of the Fort Bend site are “not competitive” with an unnamed site in another state without the JETI limitation and local abatements. The competing state is redacted in a confidential supplement.

None of the documents state a production capacity. The only big number is a quote from Elon Musk at Davos in January, cited in the application: “The SpaceX and Tesla team, both separately, are working to build to 100 GW a year of solar power in the U.S., of manufactured solar power.”

For comparison, Qcells is spending $2.5 billion on its ingot-to-module campus in Cartersville, Georgia, where a 3.5 GW cell line started production this year. Crystal Sun’s budget is four times that.

Tesla has been here before. New York spent $950 million on the Buffalo factory for Tesla’s solar business, and when the solar plans fell apart, Tesla turned to things other than solar to meet its employment requirements, including data labeling for Autopilot. Tesla did announce production of a new solar panel in Buffalo last year.

We want this factory to happen. A US plant making its own ingots, wafers, cells and modules would be rare, and it’s one of the few parts of Tesla’s business that still lines up with the mission it used to talk about before its “awesome abundance” pivot based on AI.

But corporate America loves its tax breaks. For 10 years, Tesla pays no county tax on the $8.6 billion of equipment that makes up most of the project. For the next 20, it gets 90% of it back. In return, the county’s guaranteed minimum is $2 million a year, on a $10 billion plant. And Tesla still hasn’t said it’s coming.

That’s how site selection works now. Companies file, collect offers from several states, and the county that bids the most wins. Fort Bend’s economic development director, Carlos Guzman, told ABC13 the county “would’ve been eliminated from consideration” without these agreements. Hard to argue with that, and it’s exactly why these deals keep getting richer.

That bonus for extra workers looks like an invitation to gaming (if things get that far). Paying people for make-work for a couple of years could yield tax breaks well exceeding their combined wages.

The protections are thin too. One bad year costs Tesla a year of breaks, and past rebates can’t be clawed back over a headcount shortfall, even though the county’s goal is obviously long-term employment.

Then there’s the equipment. Reuters reported in March that Tesla was in talks to buy $2.9 billion of Chinese solar equipment, and its supply chain is already caught in a patent fight between Chinese suppliers.

The state still has to sign off on the school-tax piece. After that, it’s on Tesla. Is it actually building in Texas, or is Fort Bend’s offer just leverage against the other state? From what I’m hearing, Tesla is set on the Houston-area location.

Tesla wants to start making solar panels in Texas by 2029, but you don’t have to wait that long to go solar at home. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5010. OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI Found 948 Linux Security Flaws—And Figured Out How to Exploit Them

OpenAI says its autonomous security agents discovered 948 distinct vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, developed working exploits and drafted fixes for review—all for roughly $1.2 million in API credits.

“We chose Linux to study how effectively frontier models could find vulnerabilities and demonstrate their security impact in a codebase of this scale and importance,” OpenAI said in results published Wednesday.

The Linux kernel powers cloud servers, Android devices and much of the world's critical computing infrastructure. The flaws identified in OpenAI's experiment were local privilege-escalation vulnerabilities: An attacker would first need limited access to a vulnerable machine, but could then potentially obtain root privileges and take full control.

OpenAI's agents scanned about 2.8 million lines of kernel code, or roughly 7% of the project. “Our prototype identified and analyzed 2,308 candidate Linux kernel findings, dynamically reproduced 1,724 vulnerabilities, and produced 948 local privilege escalation (LPE) exploits that could allow an unprivileged local user to gain root access” OpenAI said.

After grouping exploits by their underlying cause and proposed fix, researchers counted 948 distinct flaws.

The October run cost $1,213,430 in API credits, equivalent to around $1,279 per vulnerability. OpenAI compared that with historical $20,000-to-$70,000 payouts for Linux privilege-escalation exploits at hacking competitions, suggesting a hypothetical value of $18.9 million to $66.3 million if the same results were found manually.

An earlier GPT-5.6 Sol campaign found 166 unique privilege-escalation vulnerabilities in July for $513,690. A newer, unreleased cyber model found more than five times as many in October, even after the kernel had undergone months of fixes.

OpenAI also said its agents used at least 10 exploitation techniques unfamiliar to the researchers and outside experts they consulted—not merely existing tricks retrieved from training data.

AI companies finding critical bugs are just another proof of how systems are capable of performing advanced cybersecurity research achieving results that previously required significant human expertise.

Last week, Anthropic disclosed that an unreleased version of Claude had discovered and exploited serious vulnerabilities in widely used software, including Chrome and Linux. Some of those exploits could execute malicious code remotely with little or no user interaction.

The economics of this research were already changing before OpenAI's announcement.

Researchers at Vidoc Security reproduced several vulnerability discoveries associated with Anthropic's powerful Mythos AI using publicly accessible models for less than $30 per file.

Those tests showed that finding vulnerabilities was becoming increasingly accessible, although reproducing a flaw is different from developing a reliable exploit capable of compromising a protected system.

OpenAI's latest experiment goes further by automating much of that exploitation process, creating a potential advantage for defenders trying to patch software before criminals can take advantage of the same weaknesses.

The company warned that attackers with limited access to an outdated operating system may increasingly be able to escalate their privileges with AI assistance.

OpenAI is attempting to restrict access to its more capable cybersecurity models through verified security programs. Its Daybreak Red initiative provides approved defenders with access to specialized AI tools for vulnerability research, exploit testing, and mitigation development.

#5009. Tesla cancels Cybertruck in Australia and NZ after 7 years of deposits

Tesla has officially cancelled the Cybertruck for Australia and New Zealand. Reservation holders are getting emails today telling them their orders are void and their deposits will be refunded.

Australians started putting down A$150 to reserve the electric pickup in November 2019. That’s almost seven years of waiting for a truck Tesla now says it won’t build for them.

The email, which reservation holders started sharing on social media and Reddit this morning, is short.

“After careful consideration, plans to produce Cybertruck for Australia and New Zealand have been discontinued,” Tesla wrote. “As a result, we’re unable to fulfil your reservation and it will be cancelled.”

Tesla says customers will get a full refund for any payments made, with refunds processed within 30 days. As a consolation, the automaker is offering a free one-year subscription to FSD (Supervised) to reservation holders who order a new Model 3 or Model Y instead.

Tesla is trying to convert a dead Cybertruck reservation into a sale of a car it actually builds in right-hand drive.

The emails went out a day after Tesla reported its Q3 2026 earnings.

Australia wasn’t a small market on paper. A crowdsourced survey of reservations from 2020 put it third behind the US and Canada, with about 3% of all Cybertruck reservations. Tesla never confirmed regional numbers, but by 2023, crowdsourced tracking put total Cybertruck reservations at around 2 million.

Tesla quietly closed the Australian reservation page in May 2022 and replaced it with a “Get updates” button. It didn’t refund anyone at the time. People who had already reserved just kept waiting.

Then in 2024, Tesla shipped a Cybertruck down under for a display tour, and Elon Musk told shareholders that year: “We might be able to certify it for other markets sometime next year.” Next year came and went, and the truck never got a right-hand-drive version.

That’s the core problem. The Cybertruck is built only in left-hand drive. Australia, New Zealand, the UK, and Japan all need right-hand drive, and Australia also requires certification under its own design rules. That’s a big bill for a truck with a truck that is already having demand issues in North America.

Tesla’s few international Cybertruck launches all share one thing: they’re left-hand-drive markets. Deliveries started in South Korea late last year and in the UAE in January, followed by configurators in Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Jordan, and Israel. The truck remains blocked in the EU, mainly for pedestrian safety reasons.

And the volumes don’t support a right-hand-drive program. Tesla delivered just 8,295 “Other Models” in Q3 2026, a category that’s now almost entirely Cybertruck since Model S and Model X production ended. We estimate Cybertruck sales at 7,000 to 8,000 units last quarter, roughly a 30,000-per-year pace for a production line Tesla built for 250,000 units annually.

At that rate, no CFO would sign off on re-engineering the truck for two markets that together have about 33 million people.

This was the obvious outcome for a while, and Tesla should have said so years ago.

That’s probably hundreds of dollars Tesla has to return.

Next up, they cancel the CT completely. They sure aren’t keeping it around for the revenue or profit.

Taking deposits in 2019 for a truck that was supposed to start at $39,900 was one thing. Closing the order page in 2022 and letting thousands of people sit on refundable deposits for four more years without a word, while the CEO kept dangling “next year,” is another.

To be fair, a lot of Australian reservation holders likely already canceled their pre-orders. The Cybertruck that showed up in 2023 isn’t the one they reserved. It’s more expensive, has less range than promised, and its US sales have collapsed from about 39,000 in 2024 to a fraction of that today. Paying a premium for an import-ready version would have made it even worse value down under.

But it also confirms what we’ve been saying: the Cybertruck is now a niche, mostly American product. Tesla isn’t likely to invest more in it, and the international expansion is just about finding buyers for the existing left-hand-drive trucks.

#5006. Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

Zcash Upgrade Could Lock $16 Million in Crypto Forever

At recent market prices, those holdings represent roughly $15 million to $16 million that could become effectively frozen when the network activates its NU7 upgrade.

“NU7 (block 3,543,405) makes v4 transactions invalid, so what remains in Sprout is unspendable: frozen, not burned,” Zecstats says in its information card for Sprout.

The upgrade is scheduled for block 3,543,405, expected Thursday evening in the United States. The exact deadline depends on how quickly the blockchain processes new blocks, rather than a predetermined clock time.

Unlike Bitcoin, where transactions are generally visible to anyone examining the blockchain, Zcash allows users to conceal transaction amounts and the addresses involved. It accomplishes this through technology called zero-knowledge proofs, which can verify that a transaction is valid without revealing its private details.

NU7 changes that by eliminating version-4 transactions, the only format capable of moving coins out of Sprout. According to the network's technical specification, transactions using that format will become invalid after activation.

The decision addresses a problem familiar to anyone maintaining old software: supporting obsolete technology creates additional complexity and potential security vulnerabilities.

Developers haven't technically destroyed the coins. They will continue counting toward Zcash's circulating issuance calculations, and a future network upgrade could theoretically restore access, but that is not guaranteed, and the team has not shown any interest in doing so.

In fact, the specification explicitly warns that spending those funds may never be re-enabled.

Legacy holders are the people most exposed to the change. Anyone whose ZEC remains in Sprout must move it through a compatible withdrawal mechanism before the upgrade activates, or risk losing access indefinitely.

Sprout addresses begin with "zc," which can help users recognize old wallet records. Regular ZEC holders using modern supported wallets do not need to migrate their coins out of Sprout, although keeping wallet software updated remains important.

The blockchain also cannot reveal who owns the remaining Sprout balance, meaning nobody knows how much belongs to active users and how much is already inaccessible because its owners lost their keys.

Meanwhile, NU7 brings a benefit for everyone else: Zcash's target block interval falls from 75 seconds to 25 seconds, meaning transactions should receive their first confirmation approximately three times faster, according to the Zcash Foundation.

For Sprout holders, however, the relevant number is block 3,543,405 which will happen later today. As of the time of publication, the latest available block is 3,543,027. Once the network reaches block 3,543,405, any remaining coins in the old pool will be frozen under the new rules.

#5005. Can decentralized phone networks power the AI revolution?

Decentralized AI networks want to turn everyday phones into an alternative to centralized computing infrastructure — but skeptics have their doubts.

The sprawling data centers that underpin the AI revolution have become about as popular as baby toys made from asbestos.

Campaigners around the world highlight the massive amounts of energy and water the infrastructure requires, while markets worry about the revenue required to pay back the $38 billion cost of building a single, one-gigawatt AI data center.

But is there another way to power the AI revolution that’s sitting in your pocket right now?

As of October 2026, there are an estimated 7.68 billion mobile connections worldwide, and billions of smartphones are currently lying unused in pockets, on desks and in drawers.

Each one has a processor, memory and an internet connection, and represent a vast pool of computing power just waiting to be put to work.

So could a network of humble smartphones realistically power AI at scale?

Decentralized AI networks are emerging that aim to turn everyday smartphones into a distributed infrastructure layer, and reduce reliance on costly, centralized data centers.

Acurast says it has onboarded more than 335,000 smartphones to its network. Co-founder Alessandro De Carli says that around 40,000 of those are fully active on an average day, providing computing capacity for tasks ranging from web scraping and cybersecurity to AI inference and agent hosting.

Data center locations across the US. Source: World Resources Institute

Related: DePIN grows into a $10B sector despite token slump, Messari says

He says smartphones are particularly efficient for certain workloads. Their built-in hardware security features “excel” in areas like confidential computing or an “isolated environment” that can install and run executables. They are also well suited to workloads that are “CPU intensive,” or which require wide distribution and “unfiltered access” to the internet,

“Servers are better at multi-threading, but smartphones offer unmatched compute efficiency,” De Carli tells Magazine.

While ordinary smartphones have limited memory, and big servers retain an advantage when handling multiple tasks in parallel, he says many workloads can still fit within the memory available.

Taken as a whole, the active phones on the network “match the compute capacity of about 2,340 high-end data-center servers,” he claims, benchmarked against the ASUS RS720A-E11, a high-density server built for artificial intelligence and high-performance computing (HPC).

Mobile connectivity in 2026. Source: Datareportal

Another project, Phase, launched Phase Proxy in October, which allows smartphone owners to provide internet connectivity and blockchain validation for AI agents. Founder Kirill Eves explains that, “websites routinely block or rate-limit automated traffic originating from data centers,” so the system employs users’ mobile and residential connections as a proxy instead. Phase plans to charge 0.5 USDT per 1,000 requests and 1GB of traffic.

The next step is Phase Router which will direct simpler requests to models running on phones in the network, or route them instead to major external providers like OpenAI and Anthropic, based on “task complexity, model capabilities, performance and cost.”

The network could eventually expand beyond smartphones to desktops, servers and GPUs, he says.

But Markus Levin, co-founder of decentralized infrastructure provider XYO, says decentralized networks may be better suited to collecting and verifying real-world data rather than running AI models.

Phones and sensors can provide localized information that centralized datasets miss, he says, but only if the data is accurate and resistant to manipulation.

“A useful decentralized AI network should be judged by whether it solves a problem customers will pay to fix, not by its token price or node count,” he says.

One of the biggest challenges with mobile phone networks is coordinating the devices to provide predictable hardware, power and connectivity, in the same way data centers do.

A 2025 study tested small groups of smartphones carrying out image recognition tasks and found that clusters of phones could deliver competitive performance in certain conditions, but the results came from a controlled testing environment.

Related: Web3 companies turn to hardware with crypto-powered phones, consoles

A Google-backed project at the University of California, San Diego found that a cluster of 20 retired smartphones could handle the peak computing workload of a class of more than 75 students, with grading times faster than the university’s standard cloud setup. However, those devices were also organized in a controlled environment, not scattered across the world.

In the real world, a distributed network has to contend with phones that disconnect, overheat, run out of battery, change networks, and have varying amounts of processing power and memory.

In a blockchain network like Ethereum for example, the capacity is currently limited by how long it takes to communicate across the network, and the processing abilities of the lowest spec devices.

20 retired smartphones could handle a class of more than 75 students. Source: Google Research

Similarly, when tasks are split between several phones, slow connections or disconnected devices can throw a spanner in the works. Distributing separate, independent tasks across available phones is generally more straightforward.

Even if the technical challenges can be overcome, networks still need to make financial sense for customers and device owners alike.

Using existing smartphone hardware doesn’t incur the high capital costs of building a data center, but costs include factors like connectivity, coordination and incentives for people on the network.

When it comes to compensation, De Carli says there is “no fixed figure” because rewards for Acurast participants vary by “device uptime, hardware specifications (benchmark performance), total ACU staked and active compute demand” from developers. He says operators compete for a pool of inflation-funded rewards, rather than receiving direct payments from customers.

Phase network rewards depend on uptime, participation and customer demand.

“Phase will use a portion of its service revenue to purchase PHASE on the open market and distribute it to node operators based on their contributions to the network,” Eves says.

Running an AI model to run properly on a phone is no longer the main challenge — getting the economics to stack up while coordinating tens of thousands of phones to behave like a reliable cloud service is the real trick.

#5004. Volvo says the EX60 will be a best-seller with over 50,000 orders in Europe already

Volvo expects the EX60 to become one of its best-selling vehicles ever, with over 50,000 orders already in Europe. But as demand builds, Volvo is struggling to ramp up production.

After reporting third-quarter earnings on Friday, Volvo said the EX60 has been “a big success” so far, receiving over 50,000 orders in Europe since production began in April.

However, the company has built only around 5,000 units so far because of “new industrial technology,” Volvo said, such as the EX60’s cell-to-body structural battery design and manufacturing changes required for its new SPA3 platform.

The Swedish automaker admitted that the production ramp-up is “taking longer than planned,” but promised volumes are steadily improving.

Volvo Car’s president and CEO Håkan Samuelsson said the EX60 will be a cornerstone of Volvo’s comeback plan as one of its most profitable models, but warned that the financial impacts will not be realized until volume picks up.

After delivering the first models in Europe this summer, Volvo planned to ramp up production as the EX60 arrived in new markets, including the US.

Volvo initially expected to sell about 7,000 EX60s in the US by the end of 2026, but a dealer told Automotive News last week that the company is on pace to deliver just a fraction of that.

In a note sent to dealers in late September, Volvo said it had delayed customer deliveries in the US. A company spokesperson confirmed that EX60 availability will “remain limited” in the short term. In early 2027, Volvo expects to distribute significantly more inventory to dealers.

The delay comes at a crucial point as Volvo aims to lower its break-even point and improve cash flow as part of its turnaround plan.

As part of the restructuring, Volvo is cutting about 2,500 jobs globally, 2,000 of which are based in Sweden.

Volvo posted a loss of SEK -3.7 billion ($373 million) in the third quarter, with its operating profit margin falling to -5.6%.

“We continue to operate in a very tough environment with strong external challenges: de-globalization, hypercompetition and climate change,” Samuelsson said. He added, “In the short term, with severely challenged volumes, we need to reinforce our turnaround plan with additional decisive actions.”

While Volvo struggles to ramp up production, other luxury brands, such as BMW, are also seeing early success with their next-generation electric vehicles.

The iX3, based on BMW’s Neue Klasse platform, received over 100,000 orders after it went on sale in October 2025.

"de-globalization" is a euphemism. What they probably mean is that they can't sell the ES-90 in the US because it's made in China, and they have a hard time selling the EX-90 elsewhere because it's made in the US. That's a massive headache for a company the size of Volvo, because they can't justify having multiple production sites for those models.

BMW recently added a third shift at its new manufacturing plant in Debrecen, Hungary, just months after it introduced a second shift as it ramps up output.

Volvo is betting on its new SPA3 platform to lower production costs, boost profit margins, and accelerate its push into software-defined vehicles.

As part of its turnaround plan, Volvo is working on its largest-ever product push. The plans include 13 new cars through the end of 2030.

#5001. Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

Mercado Bitcoin Said Its Hack Was Contained, But Blockchain Data Tells a Different Story

An attacker drained Mercado Bitcoin-linked deposit addresses nearly four hours after the Brazilian exchange said it had contained a security breach, new blockchain research shows.

Bitquery's analysis, updated Thursday, traces more than $48 million in stolen assets across 11 blockchains. Its findings challenge the exchange's claim that it stopped the attack quickly.

The Bitcoin exchange announced at 9:56 a.m. Brazil time on November 2 that it had quickly contained the incident. Bitquery says 36,042 Ethereum customer deposit addresses were emptied between 9:27 a.m. and 1:56 p.m. Brazil time.

Those addresses held roughly $266,000, a small fraction of the total theft. But the attacker could still move those funds after the exchange’s announcement.

A customer deposit address works much like a personal receiving account for cryptocurrency. The exchange later gathers those coins into a shared online wallet, called a hot wallet, to process everyday transactions. Moving funds from a deposit address requires its private key, the secret that authorizes transfers.

Bitquery says the withdrawals were authorized directly by the deposit addresses, suggesting the attacker had access to their private keys or to a system capable of signing transactions on their behalf.

“That needs a key for each address, or a way into the system that signs for them,” the researchers explained. They warned that exchanges typically generate deposit keys from a single master key, meaning the attacker may still be able to access other customer addresses.

The amount stolen is also disputed. Initial estimates put the amount above $40 million, while Mercado Bitcoin's updated FAQ now puts affected assets at about $36.3 million. Bitquery estimates that $48.6 million was stolen across 11 blockchains.

Mercado Bitcoin's public updates have not explained the gap between its containment claim and the later transfers. The company assured customers it would cover user losses with its own funds.

Bitquery warns customers against reusing old deposit addresses until the exchange confirms they are safe. Mercado Bitcoin's reopening plan begins November 9 with balance viewing only; deposits, withdrawals and trading have no restart date.

#5000. Volvo is launching a ‘lower-priced’ EV in 2027 based on its SPA3 platform

The new EV will follow the EX60 as the second vehicle based on Volvo’s SPA3 platform, positioned as a smaller, more affordable option.

As part of Volvo’s largest product offensive, the company plans to launch 13 new cars through the end of 2030.

All new vehicles will be tailored to specific markets, including the US, Europe, and China. Volvo announced during its third-quarter earnings on Friday that it will reveal three of the new vehicles in 2027.

One of the new vehicles will be for Europe and the other two are destined for China (more on the US later).

Following the EX60, which Volvo said has been “a big success,” racking up over 50,000 orders in Europe so far, Volvo will launch the second electric car based on its SPA3 platform.

According to Volvo the new model is a “global BEV car, which targets a large, lower priced market segment” in Europe. The first customer deliveries are set to begin after next summer.

While the company didn’t reveal any further details, the new vehicle is suspected to be the successor to the current EX40.

The SPA3 platform supports everything from sedans and compact SUVs like the EX30 to the three-row EX90. As a mass-market vehicle, the new SPA3-based EV will likely be an SUV.

Will Volvo bring the lower-priced EV to the US? It said it was a “global BEV car,” but also that it was aimed at Europe’s lower-priced segment.

Luis Rezende, president of Volvo Cars America, told reporters during a media roundtable (via TheVerge) earlier this year that the company is preparing to launch a new EV in 2027 that will replace the EX30 as its new entry-level electric car.

Volvo initially said the lower-priced Single Motor EX30 would go on sale in the US in late 2025 starting at $34,950, but later backtracked and discontinued the electric SUV from its lineup altogether.

Although it may not be as cheap as the entry-level $35,000 EX30, Renzende said prices would be “very similar.”

According to an Automotive News report, Volvo will launch the new electric crossover SUV by the end of 2027, starting at under $50,000.

The new entry-level model is expected to go on sale as the EX50, a taller and longer electric SUV than the current EX40.

Volvo filed a trademark with the European Union Intellectual Property Office (EUIPO) for EX50 in July. Although the filing didn’t reveal many details, it did confirm it will be electric.

In the meantime, Volvo is fast-tracking production of its first long-range plug-in hybrid versions of the XC90 and XC60. The first customer deliveries are set to begin this year in Europe, followed by the US shortly after.

In Q1 2027, Volvo will begin production of the XC60 in Charleston. By the end of 2030, it plans to begin production of a “second, all-new car with multiple powertrain options,” the company said.
