#4999. The 87 Best Movies of 2026 So Far (and 17 More We Can't Wait For)

With Christopher Nolan, star-studded epic romance, and major Marvel events, this list is a behemoth. And it also features a movie called Behomoth!.

AS A HUGE fan and follower of both movies and sports, the former has one major advantage: There's always something to look forward to. As a fan of the New York Jets, I don't always have that luxury. As a fan of watching films, though, not only is there always something worth my time on the big screen or streaming, but there's always several flicks down the line that will sound great or intriguing for one reason or another. Will they all turn out great? Of course not. But with sports and movies alike, excitement tends to be all about hope.

After a particularly strong 2025—movies like One Battle After Another and Sinners proved to be true standouts—how does 2026 pick up? With strength and energy, that's how. The year has been filled with all sorts of great movies, from horror (like 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple and Obsession) to epics (like The Odyssey), to some pretty great comedies (like Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie and The Invite). It’s been a good year.

But we always like to look at not only what’s been good to date, but also what we can still be excited for—and that includes everything. We’re talking stuff on the calendar already, stuff we suspect could come out this year, and even stuff making noise in places like the Sundance Film Festival. Throughout the year, this list evolves from our most anticpated movies into our favorite movies, and always looks quite different in December than it did in January.

For now, we've got a lot we’ve loved, but still a lot to look forward to as well. Below, we've listed nearly 100 movies on our radar that we think you'll be really interested in seeing.

If you're interested in a killer chimpanzee B-movie slasher, then Primate is for you. The movie delivers what it promises. Ben the chimpanzee does some very terrible things on screen but it makes for quite an enjoyable experience.

28 Years Later: The Bone Temple is a direct sequel to last year's 28 Years Later. And while the great Danny Boyle doesn't return to direct, the movie is helmed by the more-than-capable Nia DaCosta and once again counts Alex Garland as its screenwriter. The real assets, though, are the movie's leads: Ralph Fiennes and Jack O'Connell are sensational, magnetic every minute they're on screen amidst all sorts of post-apocalyptic chaos. Bring on part three.

Here's a quick pitch for The Rip: Matt Damon and Ben Affleck. The longtime friends team up once again for The Rip, a cop thriller in the vein of movies you've probably seen several times before... but done particularly well. This twisty crime story involves a police team finding a ton of money in a stash house (the titular rip), and figuring out who may or may not be corrupt. In addition to our beloved duo, the cast is shockingly stacked: Steven Yeun, Kyle Chandler, Scott Adkins, and One Battle After AnotherAcademy Award nominee Teyana Taylor are all on board.

SAM RAIMI FANS, ASSEMBLE! For the first time since 2009, the master of gross effects and frenetic cameras is back with an original horror story in Send Help. Rachel McAdams plays a Survivor superfan who gets stuck on an island with her dickhead boss (Dylan O'Brien), and then the antics begin. Think Cast Away meets Misery meets Drag Me to Hell. It's great stuff.

Jason Momoa and Dave Bautista are a pair of cool dudes who, with The Wrecking Crew, set out and did exactly what they intended to do: Make a badass buddy action comedy. The movie finds the pair playing estranged brothers who reunite in Hawaii to investigate their father's mysterious death; From there, we get lots of jokes, and a lot of (very violent!!!) action. It's a good time! Honestly, if they want to make a couple sequels to this, I'd be her for it.

Part This Is Spinal Tap, part Josie and the Pussycats, and part Curb Your Enthusiasm, The Moment is Charli XCX's movie for anyone who enjoys her music, persona, or both. A really fun movie about music, stardom, and celebrity in 2026.

Pillion was technically released initially in 2025 (and was also included on our list of favorite movies of that year), but we didn't want to miss an opportunity to recommend it to people once again. This unique film follows a man (Harry Melling) who engages in an unexpected BDSM relationship with a biker he meets at random (Alexander Skarsgård). The movie is quite the tonal balancing act, at times funny, intense, raunchy, and heartbreaking, but it all comes together by the end. A delightful movie, and a great showcase for both leads.

The classic novel comes to life on the big screen once again, this time from Promising Young Woman and Saltburn director Emerald Fennell, and with Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi in the lead roles. Fennell, an Academy Award winner for her work on Promising Young Woman, has both big fans and big detractors at this point—but her take her, while book purists haven't been thrilled, is a big, visually stunning epic romance. Robbie and Elordi are both up to the task as well, bringing a charged energy to roles that really need it. Alison Oliver, who recently shined on HBO's Task, is another major highlight in a supporting role. An original soundtrack from Charli XCX helps to set the anachronistic mood and feels like a real cherry on top.

This action-thriller, based on a book by Don Winslow, looks like a very solid entry in the genre that we're going to call "Somewhat Resembles a Michael Mann Film." And reviews have confirmed as much—the movie has often been compared to Heat, and while its tough to compare to Mann's 1995 masterpiece, we'll take just about anything in that vein. Look: If someone tells you there's gonna be a crime thriller in the vein of Heat with Chris Hemsworth, Mark Ruffalo, Halle Berry, and Barry Keoghan leading the cast, how could you not be on board?

One of our coolest directors, Gore Verbinski, makes his grand return with Good Luck, Have Fun, Don't Die—his first movie since 2016's A Cure For Wellness. If you don't know Verbinski by name, you should know several of his movies: the original Pirates of the Caribbean trilogy, The Ring, and Rango are among the standouts. Good Luck follows Sam Rockwell as a man who claims to be from the future and fighting off a rogue artificial intelligence. While the movie takes some pretty tough and labored tangents into on-the-nose satire, Rockwell's performance and the practical effects keep the movie afloat as a fun sci-fi romp.

Despite the title, you don't need to know anything else about Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie before going to see it. Sure, this story of two inept dudes in a band who just want to play music at a bar—and go through all sorts of ordeals, including time-travel, to do it—has existed before in both web series and television series form. But you can just go in and laugh and the absolute madness of the movie. Trust us—you'll have a good time with this one if you're into "madness that makes you laugh." This is a long-term passion project of Matt Johnson, who became well known recently for directing the underrated BlackBerry. His brash talent is again on display here.

We're always in on Glen Powell, and How to Make a Killing marks his first foray into A24's world as a leading man. This black comedy neo-noir comes from Emily the Criminal director John Patton Ford and follows Powell as a man who decides to take out a few members of his family in order to increase his chance at making a whole bunch of money via inheritance. The supporting cast joining Powell isn't too shabby: Margaret Qualley, Jessica Henwick, Ed Harris, and Topher Grace are all along for the fun.

The Napa Boys is a very specific cut for comedy fans. Remember when all those nondescript American Pie direct-to-video sequels were coming out? "American Pie Presents:.."? The Napa Boys imagines itself as one of those: self-impressed raunchy comedy with little tact—crossed, naturally with the wine bro nature of 2004's Sideways. As a result, we get a movie that presents as a sequel to a franchise that both has long been running out of juice, and, in our world, never was. If you get the truly absurd brand of parody being presented here, you're going to get a real kick out of it.

Hoppers feels like a throwback to the golden age of Pixar. Not only is it an original story (at a time when the legendary animation studio has often been leaning into sequels, prequels, and more), but it's a sweet and funny one at that. Following an animal-lover who uses Avatar-style technology to transfer her consciousness into a robotic beaver body that can discover what animals are thinking and saying, Hoppers actually turns into something of a conspiracy mystery story. With a voice cast that includes Jon Hamm, Dave Franco, and Meryl Streep among others, it's a fun one for the whole family.

Look. This is one of those movies that you really don't have to overthink. It's Alan Ritchson—Reacher himself—taking on a giant alien robot monster thing. This is as much of a throwback to '80s-style action filmmaking as we're ever going to get, and it delivers as promised. The fact that the rest of the cast includes staples like Jai Courtney, Esai Morales, and Dennis Quaid is just a bonus.

Undertone has been positioning itself as A24's next huge horror hit—and we'll see how it lands. The filmfollows a supernatural podcaster who gets sent some particularly spooky recordings; Its soundscape has brought tons of hype, with many calling it the scariest experience of the year. A simple premise like that sometimes makes for a very solid horror movie.

Based on the novel by Andy Weir(who also wrote The Martian), Project Hail Mary is a story of space exploration and a regular guy (played by Ryan Gosling) who gets involved. Phil Lord and Chris Miller (who are responsible for the Spider-Verse movies and directed 21 Jump Street) are behind the camera for this one, which has already proven to be a big, big hit. Gosling is basically on his own out in space for most of this, but it also includes a fun little alien guy named Rocky and a nice supporting performance by Anatomy of a Fall star Sandra Hüller. The visuals here are worth seeing on the biggest screen you can find.

2018's Ready or Not was one of the most fun horror films of the last decade, and now eight years later we get to see Samara Weaving's lead sucked back into the same twisted, bloody, violent games. The supporting cast—including David Cronenberg (!!!), March MH Flex star Elijah Wood, Sarah Michelle Gellar, and The Pitt's Shawn Hatosy—is fantastic. The movie kind of keeps with the form of the first Ready or Not, so if you liked that one, you'll certainly have a fun time with this one.

Forbidden Fruits follows a secret coven of witches operating in a shopping mall. Like, guys, some premises you just have to accept for how good they are and see them out. The movie is a campy, fun, twisty turny ride. Understand what you're getting into—a little bit of horror, a little bit of satire, and some fun twists—and it's going to be a good time.

Zazie Beetz (Atlanta, Deadpool 2) gets to lead her own horror/thriller movie with They Will Kill You, where she plays a woman who answers a help-wanted ad for a housekeeper in a luxury apartment building, only to find herself fighting for her life. The movie is pretty similar in tone to fellow horror/comedy Ready or Not 2, which we just talked about. But They Will Kill You leans more into its John Wick-style action and Kill Bill style plot, which sets it apart. The supporting cast, which includes Patricia Arquette, Heather Graham, and Industry star Myha'la, make it extra worth it.

#4998. DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

#4997. Blockchain is ‘perfect solution’ for AI agent trust, VeChain CEO says

Sunny Lu said AI agents would need identity and reputation systems, making the case while promoting agent-building platform VeWorld.ai.

AI agents could drive blockchain’s next adoption cycle by creating demand for systems to verify their identities and reputations, according to VeChain co-founder and CEO Sunny Lu.

Autonomous AI agents operating around the clock would require a “new accountability system” to build public trust, Lu said during Cointelegraph’s Chain Reaction show on Wednesday.

“The perfect solution is a blockchain,” he said.

Lu made the case while discussing VeWorld.ai, an agent-building platform that uses VeChain’s blockchain.

In his vision, agents would have digital “passports” and operate within a “decentralized credibility system” that tracks their reputations and those of their creators. Smart contracts could help settle transactions between agents or between agents and people.

VeChain has also pursued blockchain adoption through supply-chain tracking, including food traceability projects.

AI agents could also help people turn their expertise into businesses or services, extending software-building opportunities to non-developers, according to Lu.

He compared the opportunity to mobile app stores, which allowed individual developers to distribute and monetize their work. Agent-building tools could open that market to people whose skills lie outside programming, including those with specialist professional experience, local knowledge or hobbies.

His own experiments included an educational agent for his daughter, an agent to support his mother’s daily routine and recovery, and a virtual employee to manage purchasing, inventory, sales and operations for an acquaintance’s shop in Milan.

Other industry participants have highlighted the risks of relying on agent providers.

In an October interview, Animoca Brands chairman Yat Siu told Cointelegraph that crypto traders could put AI agents to work around the clock but warned that users risked building their trading strategies around services they did not control.

If versatility is your priority, the Brooks Ghost 17 delivers. With upgraded nitrogen-fused cushioning, the latest version packs even more padding than the Ghost 16. Impressively, the higher heel stack doesn’t feel too squishy or wobbly. It’s a great do-it-all shoe for long walks, standing all day, and running.

The soft midsole offers the just-right level of give, absorbing impact without feeling too soft or marshmallowy. Plus, the breathable air mesh upper adds to the barely-there feel, and the plush ankle collar doesn’t chafe or rub at all.

This TikTok-famous sneaker reigns supreme for walking and has earned a spot in our guides to the best platform sneakers and best travel shoes for good reason: it delivers all-day comfort without looking overly athletic. The 1.5-inch platform gives this shoe major style points, elongating the legs and giving a little extra height without that heavy, clunky feel some platform sneakers have.

Plantar fasciitis and the heel pain that comes with it can quickly halt your walking workouts. The 2025 WH Fitness Award-winning, best overall plantar fasciitis walking shoe, Brooks Women’s Adrenaline GTS 24, delivers stability and support to keep you (and your plantar fascia) feeling good over time.

The New Balance 608 V5 Trainer has over 38,000 Amazon reviews, and for good reason. The brand integrated its signature ABZORB midsole, designed to reduce joint impact during walking through its cushioning and compression resistance. Plus, they come in narrow, standard, and wide sizing.

For those who appreciate a good slip-on shoe, the Skechers Go Walk Flex Azriel’s lightweight, breathable mesh upper is great for sweaty feet. The ultra-cushioned midsole also offers great arch support.

"This is a solid no-frills walking shoe that’s perfect for errands, quick hot girl strolls, or for walking your dog," says associate style and reviews editor Shannen Zitz. "I love that it slips on easily while still having the appearance of a classic sneaker with bungee laces that never come undone.”

If your main problem is overpronation or supination, the New Balance 1540v3 will lock your feet down. The brand integrates its "Rollbar stability post system," designed to prevent your ankles and feet from rolling inward or outward. On top of that, dense foam is in the midsole for added arch support, while the collar is tough for ankle security. Despite its firmness, the EVA foam remains plush. Extra-wide sizing is available to accommodate a variety of foot types (in addition to narrow, standard, and wide).

Diana Rogers, DPM, a podiatric foot and ankle surgery specialist in Crystal Lake, Illinois, and Casey Pidich, MD, a podiatrist and foot and ankle surgeon based in the Bronx, New York, both recommend Hoka's Clifton series to patients for arch support, so you know it's a good one.

Pidich loves that the design encourages great form—the sole ensures that your feet rock off the ground rather than push off your toes. Rogers specifically recommends the shoe to her patients with plantar fasciitis (a condition that causes arch pain) for this exact reason. "The lightweight feel of this shoe propels feet forward rather than making them do extra work," she says.

Lined with mesh for ultra-breathable comfort, the Pegasus is a winner thanks to its slim, low heel that helps maintain a smooth walking cadence. We love that the 42 is lighter than the previous model and has a curvier toe box that gives more toe spring, a smoother ride, and a roomier fit.

#4995. Rivian buys startup to turn 400,000 daily clips into 3D sims

Rivian wants to rebuild the roads its customers drive as 3D test worlds for its self-driving system, and it just bought a 15-person UK startup called Third Dimension to do it, as first reported by TechCrunch.

The startup was founded in 2024 by a former Tesla Autopilot simulation engineer and a veteran of Wayve’s neural simulator team. Rivian didn’t say what it paid.

Third Dimension pitched itself as a generative AI tool that builds 3D environments ready for game engines. When it raised a $7 million seed round led by Felicis in October 2024, its target customers were game studios, film, the US military, and autonomous vehicle companies.

The founders’ résumés tell you which of those Rivian cared about. CEO Tolga Kart spent more than two years at Tesla working on Autopilot simulation, after five years as a senior director on Call of Duty at Activision, and later ran engineering at synthetic data company Parallel Domain. Cofounder Piotr Sokólski spent four years at Wayve building photorealistic neural simulators for self-driving cars.

The whole team joins Rivian’s autonomy and AI group under James Philbin. Most of them are in the UK, so Rivian also gets a bigger footprint in London.

“Simulation is central to how we train and validate our autonomous driving systems,” Philbin told TechCrunch in an exclusive interview. He added that Third Dimension “has built impressive technology that turns real-world data into detailed 3D environments.”

Rivian pulls driving footage from R1S, R1T, R2, and commercial van owners who opt into camera data sharing. Philbin told TechCrunch the fleet sends back more than 400,000 sequences every day.

But the vast majority of that footage is boring. The events that actually break self-driving systems, like a mattress in the fast lane, a cop waving traffic through a red light, or a kid running out between parked cars, are rare. You can wait months for your fleet to catch enough of them, or you can rebuild real scenes in 3D and change the variables yourself. That’s the pitch here.

Rivian isn’t first. Waymo unveiled its Waymo World Model, built on Google DeepMind’s Genie 3, back in February to generate scenarios its cars have never seen. Tesla has shown off its own neural “world simulator” for FSD as well.

Rivian needs this to work fast. Uber agreed in March to invest up to $1.25 billion in Rivian to deploy R2 robotaxis, but only $300 million comes up front. The rest is tied to autonomy milestones, starting with 10,000 driverless R2s on Uber’s network in San Francisco and Miami from 2028.

On the consumer side, the timeline has already slipped once. Rivian’s point-to-point driving feature goes to a small group of invited Autonomy+ owners later this year, and those testers have to sign an NDA. “Eyes-off” driving, which RJ Scaringe promised for 2026 back in January 2025, is now a 2027 target, with Level 4 planned for 2028 on the Gen 3 hardware Rivian showed at its AI & Autonomy Day.

It is not just about video clips, it is about what the vehicle sees in the clip and what it simultaneously sees on its Lidar and Radars. The fusion of that data is what needs to get fed into the simulation model.

We don’t have all the details, but this looks like a good deal. It’s small and targeted, and it buys a team that has already done this exact work at Tesla and Wayve. For a company with a fraction of Tesla’s fleet, getting more out of every clip it collects is the right bet, and Waymo going down the same road tells you the leaders agree.

But simulation is a training and testing tool. It isn’t proof. A system can ace millions of generated edge cases and still screw up on a real street, and the only way the public finds out how it’s actually doing is when the company shares real-world numbers.

However, this is the best way to do a “simulation” because it doesn’t create a fake world with simulated physics; it reconstructs a real-world scene based on real-world data. It’s still a simulation and not as good as the real thing, but it’s as close as it gets.

If you’re buying an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #4994. Bitcoin crashes 6% to 7-week low as US stocks erase election gains

Bitcoin saw sudden downside at the Wall Street open as stocks witnessed a snap sell-off, reversing post-election gains.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell below $76,000 on Thursday as risk assets abruptly reversed their US midterm elections rally.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD shedding more than 6% to $75,463 on Bitstamp, its lowest levels since Sept. 16.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

After making swift gains on the back of a Democrat sweep of the US House of Representatives and Senate, equities saw sharp selling on Thursday as traders rotated out of tech. The S&P 500 fell 2.7%, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index lost more than 4% to hit its lowest levels since July 29.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Oil rebounded amid reports of fresh strikes on tankers in the Middle East and uncertainty over what steps Democrats may take to resolve the US-Iran war. WTI crude returned above $90 per barrel, while Brent gained 2.5% to approach $97.

CFDs on WTI crude oil one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

“We may be headed for more volatility as investors digest the election results and potential policy implications,” Jordan Watson, a portfolio manager at Tandem Investment Advisors, told CNBC. Watson added that equities gains following the midterm results should not be treated as an “all-clear signal.”

Previously, Cointelegraph reported on concerns that Democrat policies could be less favorable to the AI sector, potentially curbing already precarious capital expenditure plans.

Economist Mohamed El-Erian questioned whether the change in US political trajectory would really have a long-term negative impact on the tech sector. “Will the shift in Congress actually slow tech down? If yes (as many assume, though I’m less sure), can the rest of the market and economy thrive without the shield of tech earnings and spending?” he wrote in a blog post.

El-Erian described the day’s tech-stock weakness as “not surprising.”

Related: Bitcoin win rate vs. S&P 500 recovers as post-midterm history favors stocks

Crypto markets liquidated nearly $500 million in positioning in the first hour of the US trading session, per data from CoinGlass, with 24-hour total liquidations nearing $850 million at the time of writing.

BTC/USD vs. crypto liquidations. Source: CoinGlass

Now targeting $76,000, BTC/USD approached the bottom of its one-month trading range. The pair risked losing support at the 50-week simple moving average (SMA), currently at $75,733.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 50-week SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest analysis, crypto trader Rekt Capital noted that Bitcoin was repeating behavior from May, when a period of rangebound moves around $80,000 subsequently gave way to a breakdown and new macro lows of $57,000 in late June.

BTC/USD three-day chart. Source: Rekt Capital

#4993. Paraguay president resigns after collapse of Ueno digital bank

Santiago Peña stepped down amid mounting scrutiny over his former ties to Ueno Bank’s parent company, as the lender’s collapse left millions of customers unable to access their funds.

Update (Nov. 5, 21:21 UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from X4T founder and CEO David Eisenmann and Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino.

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday amid a deepening political crisis triggered by the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank.

In a video posted to X, Peña said he was stepping down to preserve the country’s stability, social peace and democratic institutions. He added that his priority was protecting Paraguayans who had entrusted their savings to the financial system.

Paraguay’s central bank announced resolution proceedings for Ueno Bank on Tuesday after finding that its solvency ratio had fallen below half the 12% regulatory minimum.

Peña faced mounting political scrutiny over his former business ties to Grupo Vázquez, the conglomerate that controls Ueno Bank. He remained a business partner of the group until March 2025, according to AP.

Peña has denied any wrongdoing, arguing that the controversy began as a regulatory matter before escalating into a political dispute.

The banking crisis also coincided with elevated asking prices for Tether’s USDt stablecoin on Bybit’s Paraguay peer-to-peer marketplace. One listing viewed early Thursday offered USDt at 6,942 Paraguayan guaraníes, approximately 13% above the central bank’s Nov. 4 reference rate of 6,144 guaraníes per US dollar.

His resignation came as lawmakers debated impeachment proceedings over alleged conflicts of interest, misuse of pension funds and institutional cover-ups linked to Ueno Bank’s collapse, according to Reuters.

Peña, who was elected in April 2023 and took office that August, was serving a five-year term scheduled to end in 2028. Under Paraguay’s Constitution, Vice President Pedro Alliana would succeed him for the remainder of the term if Congress accepts his resignation.

Ueno Bank had grown rapidly since 2021 into one of Paraguay’s largest financial institutions, serving more than 3 million customers in a country of roughly 6.5 million people.

Ueno first came under regulatory scrutiny in June over loans to companies linked to its controlling shareholder, Grupo Vázquez. In an October statement, the bank acknowledged receiving regulatory notices on June 5 and July 24 requiring adjustments to its related-party lending exposure.

On Nov. 2, the Central Bank of Paraguay initiated resolution proceedings after adjustments to the bank’s financial statements pushed its solvency ratio down to 5.56%, less than half of the 12% regulatory minimum. Authorities suspended the bank’s operations, restricting customers’ access to their deposits.

David Eisenmann, founder and CEO of Paraguayan crypto exchange X4T, told Cointelegraph that customers are turning to crypto to pay bills and protect their savings amid the banking crisis. Some are cashing out their holdings into guaraníes or US dollars to pay salaries and settle debts, while others are buying dollar-backed stablecoins.

“Customers are turning to their crypto holdings and converting them into guaraníes or dollars to make payments they couldn’t make with their bank deposits,” he said. “When that access is restricted, people look for other sources of liquidity.”

On X4T, USDT and USDC prices in guaraníes have risen about 8% in recent weeks, Eisenmann said, reflecting the dollar’s gains against the local currency. That recovery began in October and accelerated this week, so the increase could not be attributed entirely to Ueno Bank’s collapse, he added.

The dollar gained about 1.6% against the guaraní between Oct. 1 and the month’s final trading day, central bank data shows. Despite its rally this week, it remained about 2.8% below in 2026.

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also weighed in, writing on X that “USDt is a lifeline for hundreds millions of people in the world,” in response to Cointelegraph’s coverage of the banking turmoil.

On Thursday, Paraguay’s central bank announced that it would start deposit guarantee payments to eligible clients with balances of up to 10 million guaraníes (about $1,630), provided they had no outstanding debts to Ueno and held accounts at other financial institutions.

Under the country’s deposit insurance system, deposits are protected up to 228.3 million guaraníes (about $37,200) per depositor, per financial institution.

The bank’s collapse sparked protests in the nation’s capital, Asunción, where hundreds of depositors, pensioners and workers demanded the return of their savings and accountability from government officials.

Paraguayan authorities detained eight Ueno Bank executives on Nov. 3, including Grupo Vázquez president Miguel Vázquez and bank president Juan Manuel Gustale, according to local newspaper ABC Color. A court subsequently ordered six executives held in pretrial detention and placed two others under house arrest.

#4992. The 58 Best New Shows of 2026 (and 2 More We Can't Wait For)

We've got murder mysteries, stories of love and lust, and lots and lots of Yellowstone. And that's just the beginning.

THE IDEA OF "new" television shows is a little bit of a tricky one these days. Sure, we do still get a fair share of truly new, exciting, unique ideas each year—just look at shows like Severance a couple years ago and Pluribus last year. Shows like last year's The Lowdown—rich, well-written, character-focused stories—still draw major stars like Ethan Hawke and still hit in a big way for audiences that watch it. If someone was being dramatic and wanted to say original TV was dead, dying, or on life support, there's plenty of evidence out there to throw in their face.

But at the same time, they aren't entirely wrong. For one reason or another, so much of the TV landscape—even shows we're really excited for—are based around something that in one way or another already exists.

Look, we can't beat too much around the bush: People love nostalgia, people love things they recognize and remember, and it can be pretty hard to get anyone to even attempt to give their attention to something new, let alone get really into it in the way we all like to with the very best of TV. Which is why we kind of have two different options here—people need to be really trusting of word of mouth and really aware when something is a really good new, original show (because no one likes to waste their time with a piece of junk), and we just need to really hope that all the remakes, reboots, reimaginings, and re-whatevers are good.

And they should be! HBO's Lanterns is modeled after True Detective, stars Kyle Chandler and Aaron Pierre as the titular Green Lanterns, and counts Watchmen genius Damon Lindelof as a producer. Of course it’s good. Below, we're going to keep track all year. We're charting the shows we already know are good. We're charting the shows we think might be good. And we're charting the shows that may not even come out this year, but if they do... they still might not be good. But, again, say it with us: We're choosing to be hopeful.

Netflix has been really good of late at the pulpy, potboiler, limited series, and His & Hers is the latest in that winning streak. The show follows a detective (Jon Bernthal) and his estranged journalist wife (Tessa Thompson) as they both hunt for answers when a local woman—whom they both have a close connection to—winds up dead. Based on Alice Feeney's book of the same name, His & Hers is exactly how this kind of show should be: Juicy, fun, easy to watch, and filled with really good actors.

In shrinking its story down to a more intimate one that takes place between the margins of major universe events, Game of Thrones takes a page out of the Andor handbook with A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms (based on George R.R. Martin's Dunk and Egg novellas). This short series, which follows a big, humble, anxious hedge knight (former professional rugby playerPeter Claffey)and his little friend/squire (Dexter Sol Ansell) is a true breath of fresh air for Thrones fans on both a story and character level. It's a delight.

Doesn't the premise of Ponies just sound fun? This spy series set in '70s Moscow follows a pair of women working with the CIA who find themselves at the midst of a vast conspiracy when their husbands both die under mysterious circumstances. And those women are played by The White Lotus's Haley Lu Richardson and Game of Thrones queen Emilia Clarke. Sometimes something that sounds like a winner just turns out to be one.

The Fall and Rise of Reggie Dinkins (NBC/Peacock)

This show about a disgraced former football player (Tracy Morgan) and a documentary filmmaker following him (Daniel Radcliffe) comes from the team behind 30 Rock, so you know you're going to get several jokes per minute. Plus, Morgan and Radcliffe are no slouches themselves. The show had a special debut in January and will continue on a regular schedule later in February.

At the very least, Wonder Man is among the best shows Marvel ever made—and you could make a case that it's at the top of the list. Yahya Abdul Mateen II plays the titular hero (aka actor Simon Williams) as the kind of guy we love to love in this sort of story: humble, well-meaning, charismatic, and ambitious. But when he meets fellow actor (and former fake terrorist) Trevor Slattery (Ben Kingsley) and learns that his favorite movie from childhood is being remade by an acclaimed director, everything changes. Wonder Man isn't just a good Marvel show, but a good show about people, movies, and friendship period. Let's hope the MCU takes notes from what worked so well here and keeps the streak going in the future.

One of America's great cultural institutions made its grand return in 2026: The Muppet Show! This version—coming from executive producers Seth Rogen and Evan Goldberg—is directly modeled on the original Muppet Show, which means special guests, musical performances, comedy sketches, and lots of fun. The first episode, which featured special guest star Sabrina Carpenter, was an absolute delight for anyone who likes Kermit, Miss Piggy, and friends (or who just likes laughing and having a good time). For now, it's just this one episode, but if the reaction is strong enough (and it seems like it might be), the show will come back in full.

The latest entry in producer Ryan Murphy's American Story universe is called Love Story, and follows the romance and life between Carolyn Bessette Kennedy and John F. Kennedy Jr. The show is, however, much better than that tabloid-y description sounds; Sarah Pidgeon, who plays Carolyn, is a star, coming off her Tony-nominated role in the Fleetwood Mac-inspired play Stereophonic. But Paul Anthony Kelly, who plays JFK Jr., is a real discovery—it's his first role (he was previously a model), and he truly feels like a reincarnation of the late attorney/magazine publisher/dashing public figure. Of course, this is a story that ends in an extreme tragedy—so if you get invested, be sure to have your tissues ready.

This wild documentary series looks at different disputes between neighbors, telling compelling and often escalating true stories that will truly floor you in the depiction of their very-real truth. Think about this less as a story about the legalities of these disputes, and more a look into some larger-than-life characters through an often trippy, absurd lens. The series is produced by Josh Safdie and Ronald Bronstein, coming off of their recent success on the Oscar-nominated Marty Supreme.

The new season of Scrubs brings back almost all of the original cast, including Zach Braff, Donald Faison, and Sarah Chalke. And audiences who love and/or remember that trio and most of the show's original dramedy premise should be pleased with this new version of the beloved aughts sitcom. The last season aired in 2010, so we're OK with calling this 16-years-later revival a 'new' show.

DTF St. Louis follows trio of languishing adults—played by the electric trio of Jason Bateman, Linda Cardellini, and David Harbour—who get involved in a love triangle and general madness that results in one of them winding up dead. You may think you have an idea of what you're getting into with this show based on the cast and that premise, but believe us—all of your expectations will be upended. DTF St. Louis is a tonal balancing act, a show that bounces between hilarious dark comedy, compelling mystery, and gutting, relatable drama. It's one of the best shows of the year.

The second age of Yellowstone begins in earnest with Marshals, a 13-episode drama that airs on CBS (and streams on Parmaount+ either simultaneously or a day later, depending on which tier of subscription you have). The series focuses on Luke Grimes's character Kayce, and how an old SEAL Team leader (Logan Marshall-Green) recruits him into his Marshal squad. There's at least one more Yellowstone spinoff coming in 2026—Dutton Ranch—and so this is the one leading the charge.

One of the busiest men in show business these days—on both the big and small screen—is Guy Ritchie, and his newest endeavor takes him back to a world he's inhabited a couple times before. Young Sherlock follows, well, a Young Sherlock Holmes. And as you can imagine, it follows Arthur Conan Doyle's famous detective as he solves a mystery. Hero Fiennes Tiffin plays the titular character, and Colin Firth is also part of the show's cast. If you're into that, you'll have a fun time! It's an enjoyable show.

The erotic thriller is back! Or, well, juicy erotic character study dramedy? Whatever we want to call it, Netflix's Vladimir is one of the year's most exciting new shows. Based on the novel of the same name, this show follows a longtime literature professor at a college (Rachel Weisz, constantly breaking the fourth wall) who finds herself in the midst of controversy when her fellow professor husband (John Slattery) gets caught sleeping with students. She takes this as an opportunity to switch things up a bit, and so she becomes smitten with her very sexy new colleague (Leo Woodall). It's a fun, zippy, juicy time—and kind of like if Deadpool was actually about a smart woman in academia who's spinning her wheels and maybe just a little bit horny.

From the mold of Ted Lasso and Shrinking comes Rooster, which stars Steve Carell and counts Lasso and Shrinking co-creator Bill Lawrence (who also made Scrubs and Cougar Town) as its showrunner. Carell plays an author who heads to help his professor daughter on a college campus... and becomes enamored with life there. Imagine Back to School but with some middle-age soul-searching and academia at play? Sure! HBO's got another winner on their hands with this one—and maybe Carell can get the Emmy that he somehow never got for The Office.

There are 29 books in the Scarpetta series, and, now, one TV show on Prime Video starring Nicole Kidman and Jamie Lee Curtis. The series follows forensic pathologist Dr. Kay Scarpetta (Kidman) as she solves a crime—but the show is so much more than just that. In a way, Scarpetta is destined to fit into Prime Video's roster of rock-solid procedural murder mysteries, which also includes Reacher, Cross, Bosch, and plenty more. But it's got a little bit of an extra chaotic oomph—helped out by the star-studded cast that also includes Bobby Cannavale and Simon Baker—that make it worth your while and a unique, fun show.

Sunny Nights, which aired in Australia last year, now makes its U.S. debut on Hulu. The premise is simple: A pair of siblings (played by the very funny Will Forte and D'Arcy Carden) want to start a spray tan business, but wind up entrenched in the criminal underworld. Whoops! Forte and Carden have been two of our finest comedic actors for a while—with ability to play depth in addition to that humor—and the show thrives when they're at their strongest. A solid watch for anyone looking for something light but compelling.

This series—from Yellowstone super writer/producer Taylor Sheridan—follows Stacy Clyburn (Michelle Pfeiffer) and her family, as they move from the busy, stressful world of New York City to a more rustic setting out in beautiful Montana after a totally unforseen tragedy. Kurt Russellplays her husband, Preston, with his usual endless charisma, while the supporting cast includes the likes of Matthew Fox and Suits star Patrick J. Adams. Sheridan does his usual zigging and zagging in The Madison—the tone can sometimes shift from very sad to zany and comedic, and, as usual, there's a bit of social commentary speechifying buried in almost every scene. But if you're on board with the writing style he uses in shows like Yellowstone and Landman, you'll probably be OK with it here, especially if you're ready for a heavier and more contemplative tone. This show was originally planned to be part of the Yellowstone connected universe, but those plans have since been changed; It's now a new story, but with that Montana setting, you still get to look at all sorts of beautiful landscapes.

After striking gold a couple years ago with Jury Duty, Prime Video gives its grand Truman Show-esque experiment another try with a second season called Company Retreat. This time around, we follow a regular guy who thinks he’s working as a temp for a hot sauce company and joining them on their annual company bonding retreat. Of course, everyone around him is actually an actor in a scripted, engineered situation. It’s a little goofier than the first season, but seeing how everything plays out is compelling, often very funny television nonetheless. A worthy follow-up, and, honestly, we might just watch these as long as they feel like making them.

Riz Ahmed is the creator and star of Bait, a show about an actor who thinks he's about to get offered the role of a lifetime—James Bond!—and the madness that spirals out of such a revelation. If you're a fan of Ahmed's (and why wouldn't you be?) this will be a fun ride.

Something Very Bad Is Going To Happen (Netflix)

Camila Morrone (Daisy Jones and the Six) and Adam DiMarco(The White Lotus) are set to get married in this horror series from showrunner Haley Z. Boston (who worked on the very underrated Brand New Cherry Flavor) and producers The Duffer Brothers. And not to oversell, but Something Very Bad Is Going to Happen is the best horror show I've seen in quite some time. While the show plays with familiar settings and tropes—you may think of Ready or Not or Midsommar during the show's eight episodes—it's so filled with Ari Aster-esque dread and stunning, striking camera work that it doesn't even matter. Horror fans are in for an absolute treat with this one.

#4989. OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

#4988. Europe’s largest asset manager Amundi tokenizes equity index fund on Ethereum

The new share class offers blockchain-based exposure to an index tracking 600 companies across European markets.

Amundi, Europe’s largest fund manager by assets, has launched a tokenized share class of its STOXX Europe 600 equity index fund on Ethereum, expanding its blockchain-based investment offerings beyond money market funds.

The new share class was issued on Ethereum in collaboration with CACEIS, a European asset-servicing company that provided the tokenization infrastructure. The fund will continue to be available through traditional investment channels, Amundi said in a Thursday announcement.

The fund tracks the STOXX Europe 600 Index, which covers 600 large-, mid- and small-cap companies across developed European markets. Amundi said it plans to develop a broader range of tokenized funds for institutional clients and digital platforms, citing potential improvements in accessibility, settlement speed and operational efficiency.

The launch follows the asset manager’s introduction of its first tokenized money market fund share class in November 2025, also developed with CACEIS on Ethereum.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

Amundi’s move comes amid rapid growth in the global tokenized equities market, although the largest products remain concentrated in US-related assets.

The value of tokenized stocks reached $4.24 billion as of Wednesday, Nov. 4, up nearly 27% over the past 30 days, according to RWA.xyz data. The platform’s 10 largest tokenized equity products by value were all tied to US companies or US-listed securities, including Securitize, SpaceX, Circle and Alphabet.

Meanwhile, European financial firms have pressed regulators to accelerate reforms. In April, a coalition of 39 companies and industry groups warned that the EU risked falling behind the US in tokenized finance, calling for expanded asset eligibility, higher volume limits and the removal of time restrictions on licenses under the bloc’s Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) Pilot Regime.

#4987. Fed, OCC check US banks after Binance hack as experts flag security risks

US regulators reportedly contacted banks following last week’s $6.3 billion Binance security hack, while experts raised concerns about transaction security and crypto risks.

US regulators reportedly contacted banks following last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, as cybersecurity experts raised concerns about how financial institutions handle crypto-related security risks.

Officials from the Federal Reserve and Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) began contacting banks on Monday to ask about “unusual activity” or service disruptions, Reuters reported Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The reported inquiries could be followed by closer attention to how banks manage crypto-related risks, including custody, counterparty exposure and incident response, according to Stefan Muehlbauer, head of US government affairs at CertiK.

“After an incident of this scale, it would be surprising if the Fed and OCC didn’t check in with the banks they supervise,” Muehlbauer told Cointelegraph. “Their mandate is to protect the financial system, and that means understanding where exposures sit and how quickly they can respond.”

According to Reuters, banks have been assessing the hack and checking their systems to figure out whether they could absorb or manage similar threats.

Reuters’ report also noted that a cybersecurity team at one of the largest US banks contacted Binance within hours of the hack. A Binance spokesperson confirmed with Cointelegraph that the exchange shared its publicly released incident report with all its banking partners and was not aware of any vulnerabilities affecting them.

Muehlbauer explained the procedure currently unfolding is “how a maturing framework should work.” He added that “it’s not a signal that regulators are pulling back from the industry.”

The inquiries also come after the Federal Reserve removed a requirement in April 2025 for state member banks to notify regulators before engaging in crypto activities. The Fed said it would continue overseeing those activities through its regular supervisory process.

A Federal Reserve spokesperson told Cointelegraph that the agency had nothing to share regarding the reported inquiries. Cointelegraph also contacted the OCC for comment but has not heard back at the time of publishing.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Binance said in its Nov. 3 incident report that approximately $6.34 billion in digital assets were affected by the attack on Oct. 29, with $2.85 billion frozen or intercepted as of the report.

According to the crypto exchange, the attack began with compromised software installed by an engineer in June. Hackers later employed social engineering, stolen credentials and malicious software to get access to wallet systems and carry out unauthorized transfers.

Dr. Darren Williams, founder and CEO of cybersecurity firm BlackFog, told Cointelegraph that the methods used in the attack pose potential risks to traditional banks, particularly those offering crypto custody and payment services.

“Attackers no longer need to break encryption or steal private keys if they can compromise the trusted systems and people authorized to move money,” Williams said.

He warned that hackers could use compromised employee devices, accounts or third-party services to move money without triggering any alerts. He also said the growing use of agentic AI in financial operations could pose even more risks.

“The priority must be stopping unauthorized transactions and data exfiltration before assets or sensitive information leave the organization, not simply investigating the damage afterwards,” Williams said.

Binance has since said that it has strengthened its security controls and was bringing in independent cybersecurity experts to review the attack.

#4983. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

#4982. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#4980. Hyundai’s $359 IONIQ 5 lease looks good, but its bigger sibling may be a better buy

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 is the most popular EV in America outside of Tesla, but its larger three-row sibling looks like the better deal right now.

Through September, the Hyundai IONIQ 5 was the third top-selling EV in the US, behind the Tesla Model Y and Model 3.

Hyundai’s electric SUV is outselling rivals like the Chevy Equinox EV, Ford Mustang Mach-E, and Toyota bZ.

Why? For one, it’s one of the most affordable EVs in the US, starting at about $35,000, and Hyundai has been offering some of the most attractive lease deals.

After the $7,500 federal EV tax credit expired at the end of September last year, Hyundai responded by reducing prices on the IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims.

The 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 starts at $35,250. That’s for the standard-range IONIQ 5 SE with an EPA-estimated range of up to 245 miles. Upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model adds $2,500, starting at $37,750, and offers up to 318 miles of range.

Hyundai’s three-row electric SUV, the IONIQ 9, starts at $58,955, or nearly $24,000 more than the base IONIQ 5 SE.

On Hyundai’s website, the 2027 IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range is available to lease from $359 a month. The offer is for a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing for an effective cost of $567 per month.

The extended-range Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE RWD can be leased from $369 a month. The offer is also a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing, for an effective cost of $577 per month.

Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9, meanwhile, is available to lease from $419 a month. That deal also includes $4,999 due at signing, but is for a 36-month lease, so the effective cost is $557 per month.

Using Hyundai’s payment calculator, it would cost about $412 a month to lease the 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE for 36 months with $4,999 due at signing. With an effective cost of $550 per month, it’s only $7 less than the three-row IONIQ 9.

The Hyundai IONIQ 9 offers more range than its smaller sibling, with an EPA-estimated range of up to 335 miles for the base SE trim.

The IONIQ 5 is a midsize electric SUV, about the same size as a Tesla Model Y, with two rows and enough room for five passengers. Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9 offers three rows of seating and six- or seven-seat configurations.

With an added row of seating, the IONIQ 9 offers up to 163.54 cubic feet of interior passenger volume, compared with the IONIQ 5, which offers up to 106.5 cubic feet.

While the IONIQ 9 may be the better deal right now, the IONIQ 5 is still a good fit for those looking for a smaller, more efficient SUV that doesn’t necessarily need the added space.

Which one would you choose? The IONIQ 5 or IONIQ 9? Let us know why in the comments.

Both the IONIQ 5 and IONIQ 9 are built at Hyundai’s Metaplant America outside of Savannah, Georgia.

When we toured the facility earlier this year, Hyundai told us that its electric SUVs are attracting new buyers to the brand at a higher rate than its gas or hybrid vehicles.

The IONIQ 5 had a 69.8% conquest rate last year, while the IONIQ 9 had 64.3%. Hyundai’s Elantra N ranked third (61.9%), followed by the Palisade (61.2%) and Kona Electric (60.9%).

#4978. Binance, Deribit revoke SignalPlus API keys after fake Teams invite attack

SignalPlus said attackers compromised an engineer’s computer in a breach that resulted in losses of less than $82,000, which the crypto trading platform pledged to reimburse in full.

Exchanges Binance and Deribit said Thursday they had revoked API keys connected to crypto derivatives trading platform SignalPlus as a precaution following a security breach. Both exchanges said their own systems were unaffected, while Deribit warned that trading, monitoring and automation through SignalPlus had stopped working for its customers.

Binance co-founder Yi He also urged third-party platforms integrated with the exchange to review their security controls and monitor for suspicious activity, warning that the threat extended beyond any single company.

SignalPlus provides trading infrastructure for crypto derivatives, particularly options, allowing professional and institutional traders to manage positions across multiple exchanges through a single platform.

According to the company on X, the attack originated from a compromised Telegram account belonging to a contact at a third-party technology vendor. The attacker impersonated the contact and sent a fake Microsoft Teams meeting invitation to a SignalPlus engineer, compromising the employee’s computer.

The attacker remained dormant on the compromised computer before using the engineer’s access privileges to target SignalPlus’s internal network early Wednesday, the company said.

SignalPlus said it detected and contained the intrusion, with total losses of less than $82,000, and had begun fully reimbursing affected users. The company temporarily suspended its services and expects to restore operations over the next few days after completing security reviews and applying patches.

SignalPlus also acknowledged that it had not required IP whitelisting for exchange API access. “We take responsibility for this oversight,” the company said, adding that it would tighten API access requirements and strengthen internal security controls. IP whitelisting is a security measure that restricts API access to a predefined list of approved internet addresses.

Related: BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

The SignalPlus incident comes days after Binance disclosed details of a $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29, which also involved compromised employee computers.

According to Binance’s postmortem, attackers posing as recruiters tricked an engineer into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The attackers subsequently used the compromised computer to gain access to additional systems and eventually breach the exchange’s wallet infrastructure.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and attempts to steal employee credentials.

#4977. Crypto ally French Hill wins reelection but set to lose House chair

Republican French Hill secured another term in Congress but will lose his leadership of the House Financial Services Committee when Democrats take control in January.

House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill, a key Republican ally of the cryptocurrency industry, has won reelection in Arkansas but is expected to lose his committee chairmanship after Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in Tuesday’s midterm elections.

The Associated Press called the race Thursday, confirming Hill’s victory over Democratic challenger Chris Jones in Arkansas’ 2nd Congressional District. Hill, who sponsored the CLARITY Act and voted for the GENIUS Act, holds an A rating from the crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, which classifies him as strongly supportive of cryptocurrency.

Fairshake, a cryptocurrency industry-backed super political action committee (PAC), pledged $1 million to support Hill’s reelection campaign in October as part of its efforts to retain crypto-friendly lawmakers in Congress.

California Democrat Maxine Waters, the current ranking member of the House Financial Services Committee, is the leading contender to replace Hill as chair, according to Reuters reporting.

Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in Tuesday’s midterm elections, raising uncertainty over the future of US crypto legislation and the prospect of increased industry scrutiny. Crypto-backed super PACs spent more than $176 million during the election cycle.

#4976. GM is betting on battery cells that don’t use lithium [updated]

GM has partnered with Peak Energy to develop next-generation sodium-ion battery cells, but they’re not going into EVs – they’re for grid-scale energy storage projects. GM Ventures is making a strategic investment in Peak Energy.

June 10, 2026: Under the partnership, GM will develop the sodium-ion cells in its Michigan battery labs and retain exclusive manufacturing rights. Peak Energy will integrate the cells into its battery storage systems as it ramps up US manufacturing.

The companies are targeting a market that’s growing rapidly as utilities add battery storage to support renewable energy, rising electricity demand, and the buildout of data centers and AI infrastructure.

Today’s stationary storage market is dominated by lithium-iron phosphate (LFP) batteries. But Peak Energy believes sodium-ion batteries can deliver a lower-cost alternative for grid applications, where energy density is less important than cost, reliability, and safety.

A key part of Peak’s pitch is its passively cooled battery storage platform. Conventional LFP battery systems require active cooling equipment to maintain safe operating temperatures, adding cost and complexity. Peak says its system eliminates those cooling systems entirely.

According to the company, its sodium-ion platform can reduce energy storage costs by 20% compared with conventional systems while delivering more than 99% uptime.

Peak also argues that its design wastes less energy. It estimates that replacing conventional LFP storage systems with its passively cooled technology could reduce annual battery-storage energy waste in the US by up to 2 terawatt-hours. That’s enough electricity to power a mid-sized city for a year.

GM’s vice president of battery and sustainability, Kurt Kelty, said sodium-ion is a strong fit for stationary storage.

“At GM, we know that the application should determine the battery, and for grid-scale stationary storage, sodium-ion is the right solution,” Kelty said. “Peak is already demonstrating the value of sodium-ion through their innovative energy storage platform, and together we’re working to push those benefits even further with our next-generation cell – helping deliver more reliable, lower-cost energy storage at scale for the US grid.”

Sodium-ion batteries still account for a small share of the global battery market, but interest is growing because they can be made from more abundant, lower-cost materials than lithium-based batteries. GM and Peak are betting those advantages could make sodium-ion an increasingly attractive option for large-scale energy storage. But don’t expect GM to develop sodium-ion battery cells for EVs, because the energy density is too low.

Sodium may be the right solution for locomotive power modules as well - the harsh environment of a train won't bother them, they have great hot/cold performance characteristics and their charge cycle lifespan makes these great for automotive. Trains don't worry about aerodynamics - or really even weight. Daisy chain 2 or 3 cars worth of these behind an electric engine or two and do full swap outs for recharge/refuel stops.

The sodium-ion battery startup plans to use the money to expand US manufacturing, fulfill more than $1 billion in existing customer orders, and bring its passively cooled energy storage systems to the power-hungry data center market.
